# Report

Generated by `nndc report`. Checks compare each program with the original net (never the target); `task in / out` grades against the true task inside and outside the training range (accuracy, or max abs error for regression). Input names are shown with their real names here; the refactorer only saw `x0, x1, …`.

## How to read the table

Each model has one row per program version: `dump` is the raw network written as code, `agent_N` is
a Claude Code refactoring of it.

- **run**: `task/kind`; `hand` = handcrafted net, `seedN` = trained with that seed.
- **training**: how well the *network* learned the real task (accuracy for classifiers, R² for
  regression). "NOT learned" marks a training failure (kept, never replaced).
- **version**: which program the row is about.
- **check**: the program compared with the *network* (never the target).
  **PASS exact** = same answers everywhere, up to float rounding; **PASS snap** = differs only where
  allowed (below); **FAIL** = differs where it must not; **ERROR** = invalid program.
- **label**: how the check was done. **exhaustive** = every possible input tried (a proof for that
  domain); **empirical** = about 280k sampled inputs, concentrated near decision boundaries (strong
  testing, not proof).
- **max err**: regression only; the largest |program − network|. Snap allows up to 2% of the
  network's output range.
- **disagree outside / inside (zone)**: classifiers only. *Outside* = disagreements where the network
  was confident (must be 0 to pass); *inside* = disagreements in the network's "unsure zone" near its
  decision line (allowed). The % is the share of the input space inside that zone. The `dump` rows
  show large *inside* counts that are only float32-vs-float64 rounding ties exactly on the decision
  line, not real disagreements.
- **task in / out**: the program graded against the *true* answer, report only. *in* = inside the
  training range, *out* = up to 50% beyond it (generalization). Accuracy for classifiers (1 = perfect),
  max abs error for regression (0 = perfect). Blank *out* = discrete inputs, nothing outside.
- **recovered**: yes = the program is the true algorithm (perfect inside and outside the range).
- **nodes (% of dump)**: program size in syntax-tree nodes, and as a share of the dump.
- **audit**: Claude runs only. **ok** = the transcript shows no file access outside the blind folder;
  **OUTSIDE PATHS** = it touched outside files (such runs are excluded from headline numbers);
  **no transcript** = could not be audited.
- **Claude**: time taken and input+output tokens (input includes cached context).


## Where the refactored program beats the network

Runs where a refactored program is closer to the *true task* than the network it was decompiled from (better inside or outside the training range, and not worse on either). The refactorer never saw the target; this is the "snapping recovers the intended algorithm" effect.

## Formula vs net

The intended formula of each task (`formula.py`), checked against the net like any other version but never used to harden the other checks. A FAIL means the net rounds or shifts the formula beyond the checker's tolerance; the cases below show where.

## Kink merge (ast3)

The kink-merge route turns each sigmoid into straight pieces, merges the kinks that lie on one line, keeps the strongest lines and refits them to the net (`ast3`). Held-out runs are listed in `heldout.json` (frozen at `38b1e15`); the other runs are development runs. Only runs where the route applies (one sigmoid or tanh layer, not argmax) are listed. *kinks → lines → nearly cancelled → kept* are the counts for the kink-merge candidate's merge tolerance; where another route won, its lines are marked *candidate only* and are not in `ast3.py`.

- **held-out**: 15 of 15 pass, kink-merge won 5, recovered the formula in 6; reproduces Claude's best valid attempt in 0 of 0
- **development**: 30 of 30 pass, kink-merge won 11, recovered the formula in 11; reproduces Claude's best valid attempt in 13 of 19

## Reproduction (ast3)

Does the AST tool `ast3` (frozen at `38b1e15`) reproduce Claude's best valid attempt? Reproduces = passes the check, same recovered status as Claude, and at most max(1.5x, +5) of Claude's nodes. Held-out runs are listed in `heldout.json` and were built after the freeze.

- **held-out**: 15 of 15 pass; recovered the formula in 6; reproduces Claude in 0 of 0 with a valid attempt
- **development**: 33 of 33 pass; recovered the formula in 11; reproduces Claude in 13 of 19 with a valid attempt

## Real datasets

Dataset runs (Iris) have no known formula: their task columns below compare with the label of the nearest table row, and `recovered` is n/a. They are left out of the step-3 reproduction and the beats-the-network list above. Their results and held-out runs are in [runs_sigmoid/iris/REPORT.md](runs_sigmoid/iris/REPORT.md).

## absdiff/seed0: dump vs agent_1

```python
# dumped from a neural network: 2 inputs -> 8 -> 1 outputs
# inputs: a: float [-3, 3], b: float [-3, 3]
# output: regression: the returned value
def net(a, b):
    h1_0 = sigmoid(1.5760342 * a + 1.5582697 * b + 1.9079688)  # layer 1 neuron 0
    h1_1 = sigmoid(-1.918844 * a - 1.9395111 * b + 1.2231603)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    h1_2 = sigmoid(-1.1844841 * a + 1.1826217 * b - 4.360774)  # layer 1 neuron 2
    h1_3 = sigmoid(-2.335223 * a + 2.3351378 * b - 2.3598416)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    h1_4 = sigmoid(2.1175911 * a - 2.1213229 * b - 2.2691188)  # layer 1 neuron 4
    h1_5 = sigmoid(-1.670568 * a - 1.6375492 * b + 1.6302974)  # layer 1 neuron 5
    h1_6 = sigmoid(1.2869285 * a - 1.2854285 * b - 4.8206944)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = sigmoid(1.386017 * a + 1.3439522 * b + 1.0225431)  # layer 1 neuron 7
    return -0.19723664 * h1_0 + 0.14666697 * h1_1 + 3.9260743 * h1_2 + 1.9408692 * h1_3 + 2.1633446 * h1_4 - 0.0724625 * h1_5 + 3.6088986 * h1_6 + 0.26983872 * h1_7 - 0.27117229
```

```python
def net(a, b):
    dist = abs(b - a)
    if dist < 1:
        return 0.25 + 0.75 * dist * dist
    return min(dist, 3 + 0.4 * dist)
```

AST tool (no LLM, `ast3`):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: kink-merge)
def net(a, b):
    return -0.48620289496091235 * a + 0.48311307712501206 * b + 2.6 + 1.24 * relu(a - b + 0.25) + 0.78 * relu(a - b - 0.4) - 0.49 * relu(a - b - 4.875) - 0.525814333264346 * relu(a - b + 5)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network computes

Approximately the distance between the two inputs, `|x1 - x0|`, rounded off near 0 and flattened at
the far ends of the range.

Final program (`agent.py`, PASS (snap), max error 0.096 against tolerance 0.104):

    dist = abs(x1 - x0)
    if dist < 1:  return 0.25 + 0.75 * dist * dist     # smooth bottom, continuous at dist = 1
    return min(dist, 3 + 0.4 * dist)                    # identity, then slope 0.4 above dist = 5

#### How I got there

1. **Grouped hidden units by direction.** All 8 sigmoid units use one of two directions:
   - `x0 + x1` (units 0, 1, 5, 7). Their output weights are small (|w| < 0.3) and they almost cancel.
     Along `x0 + x1` the output changes by only about 0.01, so I replaced them with a constant.
   - `x1 - x0` (units 2, 3, 4, 6). These carry big weights (2 to 3.9) and come in mirrored pairs:
     (2, 6) with slope about 1.2 and offset about 4.6, and (3, 4) with slope about 2.2 and offset about 2.3.
     A mirrored pair `sig(a*d - b) + sig(-a*d - b)` is an even function of `d`, so the output is a
     function of `|d|`.
2. **Profiled the 1-D function** `g(|x1 - x0|)` (min/max over the orthogonal direction). It follows
   `|d|` with wiggles of about ±0.09 (the sigmoid "staircase"), plus two deviations: g(0) ≈ 0.25
   instead of 0, and it saturates above |d| ≈ 5 (g(6) ≈ 5.4).
3. **Minimax piecewise fits** (LP over sampled points) of the form `c + a*t + sum b_k*relu(t - k)`.
   The middle segment came out as exactly `t` (slope 1, intercept 0). A linear segment near 0 could
   not follow the curvature (error 0.10 to 0.19 at t ≈ 0.4 to 0.75). A parabola `0.25 + 0.75*t^2` fits
   the bottom and meets `t` at t = 1 with no jump. For the tail, the line `3 + 0.4*t` meets `t` at t = 5.
4. The remaining error, 0.096, is the sigmoid wiggle at |d| ≈ 4.35 (net is above `|d|`). This is in
   the network itself and cannot be removed by any simple formula of the kind used here.

Removed: all 4 units along `x0 + x1`, the output bias, and all sigmoids.

#### Heuristics

- **Group hidden units by weight direction** (normalise each input-weight vector). Units that share a
  direction collapse to a 1-D function of that projection. Here it was two directions, `x0+x1` and
  `x1-x0`.
- **Check the size of each direction's contribution before modelling it.** A direction whose units
  have small output weights that partly cancel can often become a constant. Measure the output
  range along that direction with the other direction held fixed.
- **Mirrored sigmoid pairs** `w1*sig(a*d - b) + w2*sig(-a'*d - b')` with similar parameters make an
  even function of d, so work with `abs(d)`. Several such pairs at increasing offsets approximate
  `|d|` as a staircase.
- **Profile, then fit minimax** (LP with hinge features at round knots) rather than least squares,
  because acceptance is a max-error test. Snap the fitted coefficients, and then look at what the
  pieces reduce to: here the middle piece became exactly `t`.
- **Sigmoid nets that approximate a kinked target** (like abs) round off the kink. Model the rounded
  part with a parabola that meets the linear part continuously (`0.25 + 0.75*t^2` meets `t` at
  t = 1), not with extra line segments.
- **Sigmoid nets saturate at the edges of the input domain.** Expect the far ends of the range to
  flatten, and add a `min(…, a + b*t)` tail segment.
- **Some error cannot be removed.** The leftover error is the staircase wiggle of the summed
  sigmoids. Its size (about 2*w/8 for steps of weight w) sets a floor for any clean formula, so
  check early that it is below the snap tolerance.

</details>

## absdiff/seed1: dump vs agent_1

```python
# dumped from a neural network: 2 inputs -> 8 -> 1 outputs
# inputs: a: float [-3, 3], b: float [-3, 3]
# output: regression: the returned value
def net(a, b):
    h1_0 = sigmoid(1.4714818 * a - 1.4191324 * b - 5.7235374)  # layer 1 neuron 0
    h1_1 = sigmoid(1.0183687 * a + 1.4039124 * b + 0.8537057)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    h1_2 = sigmoid(-1.4751775 * a - 1.3618672 * b + 1.5963116)  # layer 1 neuron 2
    h1_3 = sigmoid(1.3925903 * a + 1.3879222 * b + 1.3230511)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    h1_4 = sigmoid(0.72357696 * a + 0.6559546 * b - 0.012077273)  # layer 1 neuron 4
    h1_5 = sigmoid(1.7184958 * a - 1.668068 * b - 1.8681703)  # layer 1 neuron 5
    h1_6 = sigmoid(-1.3814204 * a + 1.4416996 * b - 5.5747676)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = sigmoid(-1.6858941 * a + 1.728565 * b - 1.8605615)  # layer 1 neuron 7
    return 3.1832032 * h1_0 - 0.084463075 * h1_1 - 0.17364007 * h1_2 + 0.26733127 * h1_3 - 0.5785187 * h1_4 + 2.9782078 * h1_5 + 3.2468553 * h1_6 + 2.932724 * h1_7 - 0.278823
```

```python
def net(a, b):
    # A smoothed |a - b|: two symmetric pairs of sigmoid steps in the difference d.
    # Each pair adds a "ramp" of height 2*weight around |d| = center.
    d = a - b
    near_step = sigmoid(1.7 * (d - 1.1)) + sigmoid(1.7 * (-d - 1.1))   # |d| crosses ~1.1
    far_step = sigmoid(1.45 * (d - 4)) + sigmoid(1.45 * (-d - 4))      # |d| crosses ~4
    return 3 * near_step + 3.2 * far_step - 0.55
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network computes

A smoothed `|x0 - x1|` on [-3, 3]^2. Along the difference d = x0 - x1 the output follows |d|
within about ±0.1 for |d| in [0.25, 5]. It bottoms out at about 0.25 at d = 0 and flattens to
about 5.45 at the corners |d| = 6. Moving along x0 + x1 changes the output by at most about 0.04.

#### What I changed

- The hidden units come in mirrored pairs on the difference direction (weights about (+a, -a) and
  (-a, +a), with nearly equal biases and output weights):
  - units 5 and 7: slope about 1.7, bias about -1.86, output weight about 2.95. That is a step at |d| ≈ 1.1.
  - units 0 and 6: slope about 1.45, bias about -5.65, output weight about 3.2. That is a step at |d| ≈ 4.
- Units 1–4 sit on the x0 + x1 direction and have small output weights (-0.58 to +0.27). Together
  they change the output by only a few hundredths, so I removed them and folded their average into
  the constant.
- I wrote each pair as `sigmoid(k*(d - c)) + sigmoid(k*(-d - c))`, with the slope k and the center
  c = bias/slope snapped: (1.7, 1.1) and (1.45, 4). The output weights became 3 and 3.2, and a
  grid search gave the constant -0.55.
- Plain `abs(x0 - x1)` fails: it is off by 0.55 at the corners and 0.25 at d = 0. Its wiggle
  error of ±0.11 is also right at the tolerance, so a piecewise-linear |d| was not worth pursuing.
- Result: `PASS (snap)`, max error 0.048 against a tolerance of 0.104. An exact pass is not
  possible without keeping all eight sigmoids.

#### Heuristics

- Look for mirrored unit pairs: weight vectors that are near negatives of each other, with similar
  bias and output weight. Their sum `s(w·x - b) + s(-w·x - b)` is an even function of w·x, a
  "bump/ramp" in |w·x|. Several such pairs with increasing centers make a smoothed |w·x|. With
  ReLU the same pattern gives an exact abs.
- Rotate to the natural coordinates (difference x0 - x1 and sum x0 + x1). Then profile the output
  along one coordinate while sweeping the other. If the spread along the sum is small, drop the
  units that point along the sum and fold their mean into the constant.
- For sigmoid units, rewrite `sigmoid(a*u + b)` as `sigmoid(a*(u - c))` with c = -b/a. Snap the
  center c (a boundary in input units) and the slope separately; centers snap more meaningfully
  than raw biases.
- Do not drop the mirrored half of a sigmoid step pair just because it looks "off" in one half of
  the domain. At u = 0 each half contributes w*sigmoid(-b), which can be a large share of the
  constant.
- The snap tolerance is 2% of the output range. Find the ideal formula's residual profile first
  (here |d| had ±0.11 wiggles plus saturation at the corners). If the profile already uses up the
  tolerance, keep the smooth sigmoid form with snapped parameters instead.
- After fixing the structure, a small grid search over snapped candidate values (with the constant
  re-fit for each) is a quick way to pick the nicest numbers that still pass.

</details>

```python
    h1_3 = sigmoid(-1.8348596 * a - 0.5835196 * b + 1.8757747)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    h1_4 = sigmoid(0.8159154 * a - 0.8151339 * b - 1.9160444)  # layer 1 neuron 4
    h1_5 = sigmoid(-1.3863059 * a + 1.4073154 * b - 5.731608)  # layer 1 neuron 5
    h1_6 = sigmoid(-3.5555162 * a + 3.5196216 * b - 3.0417547)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = sigmoid(-0.12992106 * a + 1.0350071 * b + 1.4243454)  # layer 1 neuron 7
    return 0.20720756 * h1_0 + 1.7069508 * h1_1 + 0.1652027 * h1_2 - 0.255349 * h1_3 + 5.501239 * h1_4 + 2.785724 * h1_5 + 1.6958861 * h1_6 - 0.3259363 * h1_7 - 0.32354715
```

```python
def net(a, b):
    # Smooth |b - a|: the net depends almost only on the difference d = b - a.
    d = b - a
    total = a + b
    # d < 0 side: one wide sigmoid ramp (~ -d for -4 < d < -1, saturating near 5.2).
    left_ramp = 5.9 * sigmoid(-0.8 * d - 1.9)
    # d > 0 side: three staggered sigmoid steps centred near d = 0.86, 2.2, 4.1
    # that together climb ~ +d and saturate near 5.4.
    right_ramp = 1.7 * sigmoid(3.5 * d - 3) + 1.47 * sigmoid(2 * d - 4.4) + 2.98 * sigmoid(1.4 * d - 5.7)
    # Small correction (<= 0.2) along the diagonal: slightly higher where a + b is small.
    diag_fix = 0.13 * sigmoid(2 - 2 * total) + 0.08 * sigmoid(2 + 2 * total)
    return left_ramp + right_ramp + diag_fix - 0.695
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network computes

The net is a 2 -> 8 -> 1 sigmoid MLP over x0, x1 in [-3, 3], with regression output in about
[0.24, 5.40]. On a grid it looks like a smoothed `|x1 - x0|`:
- It is about 0.3 on the diagonal.
- It is close to |d| for 1 <= |d| <= 4, where d = x1 - x0.
- It saturates near 5.2 to 5.4 at the far corners.

The output depends almost only on d. At fixed d it varies by at most about 0.17 (peak to peak),
so the best possible pure f(d) has a max error of about 0.086. The snap tolerance is 0.103.

#### Changes

1. **Grouped the hidden units by input direction.**
   - Four units have weights of the form (-a, +a) on (x0, x1), so they are functions of d:
     - neurons 1, 4, 5 and 6;
     - these carry almost all of the output (output weights 1.7, 5.5, 2.8 and 1.7).
   - The other four have small output weights (|w| <= 0.33, each contributing at most 0.33).
     Two of them (neurons 0 and 2) are functions of x0 + x1.
2. **Rewrote the four d-units on the shared variable `d`, with snapped slopes and biases.**
   - The left side (d < 0) is one wide ramp, `sigmoid(-0.8 d - 1.9)`.
   - The right side (d > 0) is three staggered steps:
     - `sigmoid(3.5 d - 3)`
     - `sigmoid(2 d - 4.4)`
     - `sigmoid(1.4 d - 5.7)`
3. **Replaced the four small fixture units** with one symmetric pair on `total = x0 + x1`:
   `sigmoid(2 - 2 total)` and `sigmoid(2 + 2 total)`.
   - Removing them entirely leaves a max error of about 0.094 to 0.097, which passes but with almost
     no margin.
   - Neurons 3 and 7 (mixed directions) gave less improvement than the x0 + x1 pair, so I dropped them.
4. **Refit the output weights and bias by minimax** (an LP over a 121x121 grid) with the snapped
   hidden units fixed, then rounded them to 2 or 3 significant digits.
   - A full nonlinear minimax refit of every parameter only reached 0.074, so the snapped form loses
     very little.
5. **Result:** 6 sigmoids instead of 8, and two named intermediate variables.
   - `check.py`: PASS (snap), max |program - net| = 0.084, against a tolerance of 0.103.
   - PASS (exact) is not reachable without keeping all eight original units at full precision.

#### Heuristics

- **Look for unit directions first.** A first-layer unit with weights (a, -a) or (a, a) is a
  function of the difference or the sum. Rewrite it on a shared `d = x1 - x0` or `s = x0 + x1`,
  which collapses a 2-D net to a 1-D function plus small corrections.
- **Measure the error floor before snapping.** Bin the net's output by the candidate variable (d)
  and take half the spread in each bin. That is the best any pure f(d) can do. Compare it with the
  snap tolerance: if the floor is close to the tolerance, keep a small correction term in the other
  direction.
- **Rank units by output-weight times activation range.** Units contributing under about 0.3 of a
  range of 5 are candidates to drop or merge, but drop them together and re-measure, since together
  they can matter.
- **Sigmoid nets that imitate |d| use a pattern:** one wide sigmoid on one side and several
  staggered steps on the other. The steps' centres (-b/k) are spaced about one unit apart, and
  their output weights add up to the saturation height. A single `abs()` will not pass when the
  ends saturate. Keep the sigmoid sum and snap k and b to one decimal place.
- **Snap the hidden layer, then refit the output layer.** Snap the hidden slopes and biases, then
  solve for the output weights by minimax (LP), not least squares. The checker uses the max error,
  and least squares left 0.12 where minimax gave 0.097 for the same features.
- **Get a lower bound to know when to stop.** A quick joint nonlinear minimax optimisation shows
  how much any further snapping or tuning could still gain.

</details>

```python
    h1_1 = sigmoid(-1.6999716 * a - 1.4470838 * b + 1.0432267)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    h1_2 = sigmoid(-2.2282135 * a - 1.8396368 * b + 0.3947982)  # layer 1 neuron 2
    h1_3 = sigmoid(1.7599554 * a + 1.9408717 * b + 1.062507)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    h1_4 = sigmoid(1.2617681 * a - 1.2578588 * b + 1.5280614)  # layer 1 neuron 4
    h1_5 = sigmoid(2.485814 * a - 2.508978 * b - 2.8118944)  # layer 1 neuron 5
    h1_6 = sigmoid(-1.8447071 * a - 1.6160655 * b + 0.8440864)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = sigmoid(-0.8145771 * a + 0.8111943 * b - 2.0779521)  # layer 1 neuron 7
    return 3.3133814 * h1_0 - 0.009013219 * h1_1 + 0.16399612 * h1_2 + 0.2512382 * h1_3 - 0.55989116 * h1_4 + 2.4631565 * h1_5 + 0.0653747 * h1_6 + 5.2271266 * h1_7 - 0.19855295
```

```python
def net(a, b):
    # The net only looks at two directions: the difference d and the sum s.
    d = a - b
    s = a + b
    # g(d): a smooth, asymmetric "|d|"-like curve built from three sigmoid ramps.
    far_right = 3.25 * sigmoid(1.6 * d - 5.75)     # extra rise for d > ~3.6
    right = 2.75 * sigmoid(2.25 * d - 2.5)         # main rise for d > ~1.1
    left = 6.25 * sigmoid(-0.75 * d - 1.75)        # broad rise for d < ~-2.3
    # small bump (about +0.09) along the diagonal when s is near 0
    valley = sigmoid(2 * s - 1) + sigmoid(-2 * s - 1)
    return far_right + right + left - 0.2 * valley - 0.65
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes on m55dec4 (2 inputs, 8 sigmoid units, regression)

#### What the network computes

Every hidden unit has weights of almost equal size on x0 and x1, so each unit reads one of two
directions only:

- difference `d = x0 - x1`: units 0, 4, 5, 7 (weights like (1.56, -1.58), (-0.81, 0.81));
- sum `s = x0 + x1`: units 1, 2, 3, 6 (weights like (-2.23, -1.84), (1.76, 1.94)).

Probing the net along these axes shows it is essentially a smooth, slightly asymmetric
`|x0 - x1|`: about 0.3-0.4 at d = 0, about 2.9 at d = +3 and 3.1 at d = -3, about 5.2 at d = ±6.
The sum units only add a small bump (about +0.09) near s = 0. Their output weights are small
(0.16, 0.25, 0.07, -0.009).

A plain `abs(x0 - x1)` is off by about 0.3 at d = 0. The snap tolerance is 0.098, so it fails.

#### What I changed

1. Rewrote all units in terms of `d` and `s`.
2. Replaced the four d-units with three sigmoid ramps in d. Unit 4 (weight -0.56) is not needed
   once the others are refit. Hidden slopes and offsets are snapped to a 0.25 grid, except one
   slope of 1.6.
3. Replaced the four s-units with one symmetric pair, `sigmoid(2s-1) + sigmoid(-2s-1)`.
   Unit 1 (weight -0.009) is dropped as noise.
4. Picked the output weights with a minimax LP on a 61x61 grid. I then searched a grid of
   0.05-step values for the coarsest set that still has max error < 0.065.

Result: `PASS (snap)` with max |program - net| = 0.058 (tolerance 0.098). There are 4 sigmoids
instead of 8.

A simpler d-only version also passes, with max error 0.086 and less margin:
`3*sigmoid(1.9d-7) + 3*sigmoid(2.1d-2.5) + 6.5*sigmoid(-0.7153d-1.7) - 0.8529`. It is very
sensitive to the slope of the large-weight unit. The best possible d-only fit has error 0.066,
because the net varies by up to 0.13 along s at fixed d. That leaves little room for snapping.
Adding the s-bump brings the best-fit error down to 0.028, which gives room to snap.

An exact pass would need practically all 8 original units, so I did not attempt it.

#### Heuristics

- **Look for shared input directions first.** If a unit's weights on two inputs are nearly equal
  or nearly opposite, rewrite it in terms of `x0+x1` and `x0-x1`. Then probe the net on that
  rotated grid (fixed d, varying s, and the reverse). This usually shows the function is mostly
  1-D, here a soft `|x0-x1|`.
- **Estimate the 1-D floor before snapping.** At each fixed d, take the min and max of the net
  over s. Half the largest spread is the best error any d-only program can reach. If it is close
  to the snap tolerance, keep a correction term for the other direction.
- **Refit the output layer after changing hidden units.** It is linear in the output weights,
  so a minimax LP (`linprog` minimising t subject to |A w - y| <= t) gives the best weights. Do this
  instead of snapping each weight separately; snapping one at a time compounds errors quickly.
- **Separate snapping hidden and output parameters.** Snap hidden slopes and offsets by searching
  a small grid of nice values, solving the output LP for each combination. Then search the output
  weights on a fine grid (0.05) and score each set by how coarse its values are, under an error
  limit of about 2/3 of the tolerance.
- **Greedy "coarsest first" snapping can throw away a useful term.** It snapped a small weight to
  0 and lost the term that made the snapping possible. Score coarseness together with error, or
  search jointly.
- **Watch large output weights on shallow sigmoids.** A unit like `6.5*sigmoid(-0.7d-1.7)` over
  |d| <= 6 makes the error very sensitive to the slope (0.01 in slope moves the error by about
  0.04). Snap such units early and let the other parameters absorb the change.
- **Symmetric bumps** across a direction can be written as
  `k*(sigmoid(a*s+b) + sigmoid(-a*s+b))`, one shared pair instead of several one-sided units.
- **Drop units with tiny output weights** (here |w| < 0.01) at once. They are below any
  tolerance.
- **Check the probe yourself before trusting a "nice formula" guess.** `abs(x0-x1)` looked right
  but was 3x over tolerance at d = 0, where the net smooths out the corner.

</details>

```python
# dumped from a neural network: 2 inputs -> 8 -> 1 outputs
# inputs: a: float [-3, 3], b: float [-3, 3]
# output: regression: the returned value
def net(a, b):
    h1_0 = sigmoid(-0.052513428 * a - 0.22641179 * b + 1.3441563)  # layer 1 neuron 0
    h1_1 = sigmoid(0.09996305 * a - 0.07863644 * b + 1.2677885)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    h1_2 = sigmoid(0.04891198 * a - 0.17902395 * b + 0.9946669)  # layer 1 neuron 2
    h1_3 = sigmoid(0.710797 * a - 0.7152358 * b - 1.7827095)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    h1_4 = sigmoid(0.19357155 * a - 0.005729861 * b + 1.1464425)  # layer 1 neuron 4
    h1_5 = sigmoid(1.2538643 * a - 1.253644 * b + 4.500368)  # layer 1 neuron 5
    h1_6 = sigmoid(0.29027292 * a - 0.15421104 * b + 0.52731156)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = sigmoid(-2.5734644 * a + 2.5779605 * b - 2.6494086)  # layer 1 neuron 7
    return 1.0139246 * h1_0 + 0.8933184 * h1_1 + 0.9775807 * h1_2 + 5.9141073 * h1_3 + 1.0562305 * h1_4 - 4.4847927 * h1_5 + 0.9540187 * h1_6 + 2.4610639 * h1_7 + 0.12999307
```

```python
# A smoothed |a - b|: the net only looks at the difference d = a - b.
# Three soft steps in d build the "V":
#   rise_pos : turns on for d > 2.4 (right arm),
#   rise_neg : turns on for d < -1  (left arm, inner part),
#   rise_far : turns on for d < -3.6 (left arm, outer part),
# plus a small slope and an offset.
def net(a, b):
    d = a - b
    rise_pos = sigmoid(0.7 * (d - 2.4))
    rise_neg = sigmoid(-2.5 * (d + 1))
    rise_far = sigmoid(-1.2 * (d + 3.6))
    return 6 * rise_pos + 2.5 * rise_neg + 4.5 * rise_far + 0.1 * d - 0.9
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does

The output depends almost only on `d = x0 - x1`. It is a smoothed, slightly lopsided `|x0 - x1|`:
about 0.3 at d = 0, about 0.9 at |d| = 1, about 2 at |d| = 2, about 4.07 at |d| = 4, and it flattens
toward 5.2-5.3 at |d| = 6.

#### What I changed

1. **Grouped the hidden units by direction.** Units 3, 5 and 7 have weights `(w, -w)` (0.71/-0.72,
   1.254/-1.254, -2.573/2.578), so they are functions of `d = x0 - x1`. They are the three soft steps
   that make the "V".
2. **Collapsed the five weak units (0, 1, 2, 4, 6).** Their pre-activation slopes are small (|w| <= 0.3)
   and they stay in a near-linear part of the sigmoid. Together they are about `0.115*x0 - 0.117*x1 + 3.59`,
   which is again a function of `d` (about `0.116*d`). They were absorbed into a linear term plus the offset.
   Their remaining curvature (about -0.0045*(x0^2+x1^2)) is the only part that is not a function of `d`.
   It spreads the output by about +-0.035 at a fixed `d`, which no function of `d` alone can remove.
3. **Wrote each step as `sigmoid(s * (d - k))`** so that each step has a slope `s` and a centre `k`:
   centres 2.454 -> 2.4, -3.58 -> -3.6, -1.02 -> -1; slopes 0.718 -> 0.7, 1.262 -> 1.2, 2.589 -> 2.5.
   I flipped the negative-weight unit with `-w*sigmoid(z) = w*sigmoid(-z) - w`, so all three steps now
   add to the output.
4. **Snapped the output weights** to 6, 2.5 and 4.5, the slope to 0.1 and the offset to -0.9.
   I picked these with a grid search over nice values. It minimised the max error against the net's
   per-`d` min/max envelope, sampled on a 241x241 grid.

Result: `PASS (snap)`, max |program - net| = 0.065 against a tolerance of 0.101.
The worst points are near the x1 = -3 edge. An exact pass would need all 8 units.
The unexplained 2D spread alone is about 0.035, and the best possible fit with 3 free steps in d
is about 0.037.

#### Heuristics

- **Look for units with weights `(w, -w)` (or a common ratio).** They depend on one combined input such
  as `x0 - x1`. When all the strong units share a direction, reduce the problem to one variable.
- **Weak units are linear plus a constant.** A sigmoid unit with |pre-activation slope| * input range
  well below about 4 stays in its near-linear zone. Fit `a.x + c` to their summed contribution on a grid.
  Check the max residual before you drop them; here linear alone left 0.09, which used most of the
  budget until it was merged with the rest of the fit.
- **Rewrite `sigmoid(w*x + b)` as `sigmoid(s*(x - k))` with `k = -b/w`.** Centres and slopes are easier
  to snap than raw biases. A negative output weight can be turned positive with
  `-a*sigmoid(z) = a*sigmoid(-z) - a`.
- **Fit against the per-coordinate envelope, not the mean.** When the reduced variable leaves some
  spread, bin the outputs by `d` and record min and max per bin. Minimax fitting against `[lo, hi]` gives the true
  max error: for each bin it is max(fit - lo, hi - fit). Get those inequalities the right way round: a sign slip
  made the error look like it was 0.001.
- **Snap in stages.** First snap the nonlinear parameters (centres, slopes) to a grid. Solve the linear
  output weights by LP (minimax). Then round those weights to nice values and re-centre the constant as the
  midpoint of the residual range.
- **Leave margin.** The checker samples edges and corners, where the error was about 0.007 larger than on
  my grid. Aim to use at most about 60% of the snap tolerance.
- **Piecewise-linear is not always simpler.** A smooth V needed 5 or more ReLU knots to reach the
  tolerance, while 3 sigmoid steps in `d` fit it with lots of room.

</details>

```python
def net(a, b):
    h1_0 = sigmoid(-0.9675831 * a - 0.9218849 * b + 1.0159254)  # layer 1 neuron 0
    h1_1 = sigmoid(1.0515056 * a - 0.9950888 * b - 1.2737435)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    h1_2 = sigmoid(1.1077025 * a - 1.1041028 * b - 4.012792)  # layer 1 neuron 2
    h1_3 = sigmoid(-1.5625771 * a - 1.5699453 * b + 1.4376283)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    h1_4 = sigmoid(-2.043423 * a + 2.054188 * b - 2.1983342)  # layer 1 neuron 4
    h1_5 = sigmoid(2.671079 * a - 2.664358 * b - 2.5020027)  # layer 1 neuron 5
    h1_6 = sigmoid(-1.3332583 * a + 1.3414316 * b - 5.028585)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = sigmoid(1.9078987 * a + 1.9712238 * b + 0.7066641)  # layer 1 neuron 7
    return 0.11193924 * h1_0 + 0.37809524 * h1_1 + 4.0686545 * h1_2 - 0.034991324 * h1_3 + 2.3091571 * h1_4 + 1.5548137 * h1_5 + 3.5025601 * h1_6 + 0.029824596 * h1_7 - 0.34955162
```

```python
def net(a, b):
    # The net depends (almost) only on the distance between the inputs.
    dist = abs(a - b)
    # Two smooth steps that together trace a ramp ~ dist on [0, 6]:
    # a step of 1.75 centred at dist = 1.1, and a step of 3.8 centred at dist = 3.7.
    near_step = sigmoid(2.5 * (dist - 1.1))
    far_step = sigmoid(1.25 * (dist - 3.7))
    return 0.07 + 1.75 * near_step + 3.8 * far_step
```


<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does

- The 8 hidden sigmoid units split into two groups by input direction:
  - 5 units with weights (w, -w) on (x0, x1), so they only see `d = x0 - x1`. Three face +d
    (centres d ~ 0.94, 1.21, 3.63) and two face -d (centres d ~ -1.07, -3.75).
  - 3 units with weights (w, w), so they only see `s = x0 + x1`. Their output weights are tiny
    (0.11, -0.035, 0.03): together they vary by only ~0.047 over the domain (about 1% of the output range).
- Along d, the output is a smoothed ramp close to `|x0 - x1|`. It is ~0.25 at d = 0, tracks |d| within
  about ±0.09 for |d| in [1, 5], and bends down to ~5.4 at |d| = 6. The two sides of d differ by at most ~0.035.
  So the net is a soft "absolute difference" of the inputs.
- Plain `abs(x0 - x1)` does not pass: it is off by 0.25 at d = 0 and by 0.6 at the corners, and the
  tolerance is 0.104.

#### What I changed

1. Dropped the 3 s-units and folded their mean into the constant. The cost is about ±0.024.
2. Made the model symmetric in d and wrote it in terms of `dist = abs(x0 - x1)`. On each side, the
   units pair up into two steps: one near |d| ~ 1.1 (net weights 1.55+0.38 on +d and 2.31 on -d) and one
   near |d| ~ 3.7 (4.07 on +d and 3.50 on -d).
3. Fitted `c + A*sigmoid(w1*(dist - t1)) + B*sigmoid(w2*(dist - t2))` to the net's envelope over s.
   For each d, I took the min and max of the net over all valid s, then did a minimax fit. After that
   I grid-searched snapped values: w1 = 2.5, t1 = 1.1, w2 = 1.25 (half of w1), t2 = 3.7, A = 1.75,
   B = 3.8, c = 0.07.
4. The result has 2 sigmoids instead of 8, and every number is a short decimal.
   Check: `PASS (snap)`, max |program - net| = 0.049 against a tolerance of 0.104.

Final program:

```python
dist = abs(x0 - x1)
return 0.07 + 1.75 * sigmoid(2.5 * (dist - 1.1)) + 3.8 * sigmoid(1.25 * (dist - 3.7))
```

#### Heuristics

- **Group hidden units by weight direction.** Normalise each unit's input-weight vector. Units with
  (w, -w) only see x0 - x1, and units with (w, w) only see x0 + x1. That turns a 2-D net into a sum of 1-D
  functions of a few projections.
- **Measure a group's total swing before keeping it.** Multiply each output weight by the
  sigmoid's actual variation over the domain. A group whose swing is well under the snap tolerance
  becomes its mean, folded into the bias.
- **Look for mirrored units, then use abs.** Units that face +d and -d with similar centres and
  similar summed amplitudes form a symmetric function of d. Rewriting it as f(|d|) halves the unit count.
  Check the asymmetry first, by comparing net(d) with net(-d), so you know how much of the budget it uses.
- **Several sigmoids in a staircase usually approximate a ramp** (here, |d|). Compare the net with the
  simple formula. If the formula alone fails, because of the soft corner at 0 and saturation at the
  domain edge, keep a small number of steps and refit them instead of forcing the ideal formula.
- **Fit to the envelope, not to samples.** For a model in one projection, compute for each value of
  that projection the min and max of the net over the remaining directions. Then do a minimax fit to
  (lo+hi)/2 with error |f - mid| + half-spread. It is fast (1-D) and matches the checker's max-error criterion.
- **Snap in a grid search.** Fix slopes to nice values (2.5, 1.25), then grid-search centres and
  amplitudes in steps of 0.05–0.1. The constant has a closed form: (max(hi-f) + min(lo-f))/2.
  Sigmoid-sum fits have nearly flat valleys, so sequential snap-and-refit wanders. A small grid over
  rounded values works better.
- **Write sigmoid arguments as `w * (x - centre)`**, so the threshold can be read directly.

</details>

```python
def net(sepal_length, sepal_width, petal_length, petal_width):
    # class 0 vs 1: a plane, mostly petal_length and petal_width against sepal_width (sepal_length barely matters)
    size01 = petal_length + 0.875 * petal_width - 0.8 * sepal_width - 0.025 * sepal_length
    if size01 < 0.7:
        return 0
    # class 1 vs 2: a slightly curved surface (petal_width enters with a small quadratic term)
    size12 = petal_width - 0.03 * petal_width * petal_width + 0.525 * petal_length - 0.375 * sepal_width - 0.2 * sepal_length
    if size12 < 1.855:
        return 1
    return 2
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### Result

`agent.py` is a two-step decision list that replaces the 4-8-3 sigmoid network:

```
size01 = x2 + 0.875*x3 - 0.8*x1 - 0.025*x0      -> class 0 if size01 < 0.7
size12 = x3 - 0.03*x3*x3 + 0.525*x2 - 0.375*x1 - 0.2*x0   -> class 1 if size12 < 1.855, else 2
```

`check.py`: PASS (snap), 0 disagreements where the net is confident, about 4.5k in the unsure zone
(2.86% of the domain). I also re-ran the checker's input-set builder with seeds 1 to 5 (fresh random,
boundary and corner points). All passed with 0 confident disagreements, so the fit is not tuned only to
the seed-0 points. PASS (exact) is out of reach without keeping the sigmoids: even the dump itself
differs from the float32 net in the unsure zone.

#### What I did

1. **Class layout.** Over the box, class 0 is 22%, class 1 57% and class 2 22%. Classes 0 and 2 never
   meet: on class-2 points the 0-vs-1 plane score stays below -17, and on class-0 points the 1-vs-2
   score stays above +14. So argmax over three logits becomes a chain: test 0 vs 1, then test 1 vs 2.
2. **0 vs 1 boundary.** I fitted a plane to points bisected onto `o0 = o1` (|o0-o1| < 0.05). The
   residual std was 0.006 in x2 units, against a zone half-width of about 0.046, so the boundary is
   planar. I then grid-searched snapped coefficients, keeping x2's coefficient at 1. For each
   candidate I took the interval of intercepts that separates all confident points. x0's coefficient
   cannot be 0: no feasible intercept exists. 0.025 (= 1/40) works. With x1 at 0.8 and x3 at 0.875,
   the intercept interval is (0.66, 0.714), and I chose 0.7.
3. **1 vs 2 boundary.** No plane separates the confident points; the best one leaves about 20
   mismatches. The plane residuals had a U shape along x3: they were positive at both ends, at low
   and at high x3. I added each quadratic term on its own and fitted the implicit surface
   `x3 = lin(x0,x1,x2) + q`. Only `x3*x3` brought the mismatches down to almost 0. A grid search over
   snapped values (k for x3², a0, a1, a2) found k = 0.03, a0 = 0.2, a1 = 0.375, a2 = 0.525, with a
   feasible intercept interval of (1.848, 1.863). I chose 1.855. `x3 - 0.03 x3²` is monotone on
   [0.1, 2.5], so the surface is still a single sheet.
4. **Removed** all 8 hidden sigmoid units, all three output logits and the argmax.

Reading the result: x2 and x3 (with x1 counted against them) behave like a single "size" measure. Class
0 is small, class 1 is medium and class 2 is large. x0 matters only for the 1/2 split, and only a
little there.

#### Heuristics

- **Look for a decision list in argmax nets.** Measure each class pair's logit difference on the other
  classes' regions. If one pair never shares a boundary (its difference stays far from 0), the
  argmax is a chain of binary tests. Order the tests by class adjacency.
- **Fit each pairwise boundary separately.** Bisect points onto `o_i - o_j = 0`, regress one input on
  the others and compare the residual with the zone half-width (margin / |gradient| along that
  input). If the residual is well inside the half-width, a plane will do.
- **Snap by intercept interval, not least squares.** For each snapped coefficient vector, compute
  max(score on negative points) and min(score on positive points) over the confident points. A
  positive gap means a valid intercept exists; prefer the widest gap, then pick a round intercept
  inside it. This search is cheap and gives zero confident errors by construction.
- **For a curved boundary, find the curvature first.** Bin the plane residuals by each input. A U
  shape in one input points to that input's square term, here x3². Test single quadratic terms
  before adding several.
- **Do not drop a small coefficient without checking it.** Its effect is coefficient × input range,
  compared with the zone width: 0.025 × 3.6 = 0.09 is about twice the half-width of 0.046 here.
- **Check robustness on other seeds.** Re-run the checker's input-set builder with other seeds.
  Snapped intercepts chosen near the edge of a narrow feasible interval can overfit one sample.

</details>

```python
# dumped from a neural network: 4 inputs -> 8 -> 3 outputs
# inputs: sepal_length: float [4.3, 7.9], sepal_width: float [2, 4.4], petal_length: float [1, 6.9], petal_width: float [0.1, 2.5]
# output: argmax over 3 outputs: the returned class index
def net(sepal_length, sepal_width, petal_length, petal_width):
    h1_0 = sigmoid(0.44006774 * sepal_length + 0.8783812 * sepal_width - 1.3263097 * petal_length - 1.7605765 * petal_width + 3.6185844)  # layer 1 neuron 0
    h1_1 = sigmoid(0.48591796 * sepal_length + 1.597092 * sepal_width - 1.6372896 * petal_length - 2.114304 * petal_width + 3.0369208)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    h1_2 = sigmoid(-0.31034657 * sepal_length + 0.07434526 * sepal_width + 0.4919914 * petal_length + 2.0065358 * petal_width - 3.8383493)  # layer 1 neuron 2
    h1_3 = sigmoid(0.6898448 * sepal_length + 1.2883805 * sepal_width - 1.6567682 * petal_length - 2.4484668 * petal_width + 3.4487345)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    h1_4 = sigmoid(-0.55830806 * sepal_length - 1.3794118 * sepal_width + 2.3196416 * petal_length + 2.1776814 * petal_width - 0.67389286)  # layer 1 neuron 4
    h1_5 = sigmoid(0.6519893 * sepal_length + 1.3597285 * sepal_width - 2.5582235 * petal_length - 2.762464 * petal_width + 1.2170631)  # layer 1 neuron 5
    h1_6 = sigmoid(-0.24189551 * sepal_length - 1.2214395 * sepal_width + 1.6514777 * petal_length + 1.6370405 * petal_width - 0.7560317)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = sigmoid(-0.7985509 * sepal_length - 0.89695597 * sepal_width + 1.5751405 * petal_length + 2.5012631 * petal_width - 3.7096436)  # layer 1 neuron 7
    o0 = 2.5868752 * h1_0 + 1.6961287 * h1_1 - 2.282691 * h1_2 + 2.7634203 * h1_3 - 2.5628247 * h1_4 + 3.6910806 * h1_5 - 2.2833176 * h1_6 - 1.3955055 * h1_7 + 0.15472092  # output 0
    o1 = 2.6722214 * h1_0 + 2.847213 * h1_1 - 2.56007 * h1_2 + 3.2236183 * h1_3 + 2.2574213 * h1_4 - 6.3900375 * h1_5 + 1.9482106 * h1_6 - 2.707149 * h1_7 - 0.36506498  # output 1
    o2 = -3.462209 * h1_0 - 3.6644619 * h1_1 + 3.356131 * h1_2 - 3.927423 * h1_3 + 0.7509426 * h1_4 - 0.9555121 * h1_5 + 1.4393805 * h1_6 + 3.214484 * h1_7 + 0.13545278  # output 2
    return argmax(o0, o1, o2)
```

```python
# 3-class classifier over 4 measurements.
# All 8 sigmoid units point roughly along one direction (+sepal_length, +sepal_width, -petal_length, -petal_width); outside the
# net's unsure zone the three classes are cut apart by two straight boundaries:
#   class 0 | class 1   and   class 1 | class 2   (classes 0 and 2 never touch).
def net(sepal_length, sepal_width, petal_length, petal_width):
    # class 0: the petal_length + petal_width sum is small compared with sepal_length and sepal_width
    if petal_length + petal_width < 0.23 * sepal_length + 0.58 * sepal_width + 0.367:
        return 0
    # class 2: petal_length + 1.62 * petal_width is large compared with sepal_length and sepal_width
    if petal_length + 1.62 * petal_width > 0.4 * sepal_length + 0.7 * sepal_width + 3.217:
        return 2
    return 1
```

```python
    h1_5 = sigmoid(0.6519893 * sepal_length + 1.3597285 * sepal_width - 2.5582235 * petal_length - 2.762464 * petal_width + 1.2170631)
    h1_6 = sigmoid(-0.24189551 * sepal_length - 1.2214395 * sepal_width + 1.6514777 * petal_length + 1.6370405 * petal_width - 0.7560317)
    h1_7 = sigmoid(-0.7985509 * sepal_length - 0.89695597 * sepal_width + 1.5751405 * petal_length + 2.5012631 * petal_width - 3.7096436)
    o0 = 2.5868752 * h1_0 + 1.6961287 * h1_1 - 2.282691 * h1_2 + 2.7634203 * h1_3 - 2.5628247 * h1_4 + 3.6910806 * h1_5 - 2.2833176 * h1_6 - 1.3955055 * h1_7 + 0.15472092
    o1 = 2.6722214 * h1_0 + 2.847213 * h1_1 - 2.56007 * h1_2 + 3.2236183 * h1_3 + 2.2574213 * h1_4 - 6.3900375 * h1_5 + 1.9482106 * h1_6 - 2.707149 * h1_7 - 0.36506498
    o2 = -3.462209 * h1_0 - 3.6644619 * h1_1 + 3.356131 * h1_2 - 3.927423 * h1_3 + 0.7509426 * h1_4 - 0.9555121 * h1_5 + 1.4393805 * h1_6 + 3.214484 * h1_7 + 0.13545278
    return argmax(o0, o1, o2)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does
- Shape: 4 inputs -> 8 sigmoid units -> 3 outputs, argmax.
- All 8 hidden weight vectors point roughly along one direction, about (+0.2, +0.4, -0.6, -0.7)
  after normalising. Four units point one way and four point the opposite way. The first-layer
  weight matrix is close to rank 1 (singular values 8.6, 1.4, 0.6, 0.3).
- I sampled the checker's own input set and recorded which class came first and which came second.
  Only the 0|1 and 1|2 class pairs ever meet; 0 and 2 never border each other. That makes the
  classes ordered along one axis, so a two-step decision list is enough:
  "class 0? else class 2? else class 1".

#### What I changed
- I replaced the 8 sigmoids and the 3 output sums with two linear cuts:
  - class 0 if `x2 + x3 < 0.23*x0 + 0.58*x1 + 0.367`
  - class 2 if `x2 + 1.62*x3 > 0.4*x0 + 0.7*x1 + 3.217`
  - otherwise class 1
- How I got the coefficients:
  1. Fitted each cut with a linear program: separate the points where the net is confident
     (score >= the checker's unsure-zone margin), with the largest gap and the x2 coefficient
     fixed to 1.
  2. Snapped the coefficients to 2 decimal places. For each snapped set, the feasible bias
     interval is `max(-w.x over positives)` to `min(-w.x over negatives)`, and I kept only sets
     where that interval is not empty.
  3. Picked the bias at the middle of that interval.
- The x3 coefficient of the class-0 cut snapped to exactly 1 (LP optimum 1.023), so that cut
  depends only on the sum x2 + x3.
- The class-2 boundary is slightly curved. Its maximum linear gap is only about 0.012 in x2 units,
  so its coefficients could not be snapped any coarser than 0.4 / 0.7 / 1.62. Rounder sets such as
  (0.4, 0.7, 1.6) have an almost empty bias interval.
- Result: `PASS (snap)`, with 0 disagreements where the net is confident and about 2.8% of the
  domain in the unsure zone. I also re-checked against 15 other seeds of the checker's input set
  (random points plus boundary points): 0 confident disagreements in all of them.
- An exact pass would need the full sigmoid network, so I chose the readable snap version.

#### Heuristics
- For argmax nets, first find which class pairs actually border each other: sample points and
  count (top class, second class) where the top-two margin is small. If the classes form a chain
  (0|1, 1|2 only), a decision list with one cut per border reproduces the net.
- The SVD of the first-layer weights shows how many directions matter. A near-rank-1 hidden layer
  with sigmoid/tanh units means the boundaries are close to parallel hyperplanes, so try linear
  cuts before anything else.
- Fit each cut only on points outside the checker's unsure zone (margin_frac * p95 of the
  top-two score), and maximise the gap with an LP. Points inside the zone are free, which is what
  makes a linear cut possible for a mildly curved sigmoid boundary.
- Snapping with a bias interval: fix the snapped slope coefficients, then compute the feasible bias
  as `[max(-w.x_pos), min(-w.x_neg)]`. An empty interval rejects the snap at once; otherwise put the
  bias in the middle. Then try coarser snaps one coefficient at a time (exactly 1, then x.x0, then
  x.xx).
- Check robustness on extra seeds or a pooled set of inputs. A cut that passes one seed with a
  bias gap of about 0.005 can fail on another seed. Choose the bias from the pooled interval.

</details>

```python
    h1_4 = sigmoid(0.15145655 * sepal_length - 1.62617 * sepal_width + 1.1817795 * petal_length + 1.8817569 * petal_width - 1.0102587)  # layer 1 neuron 4
    h1_5 = sigmoid(0.5799047 * sepal_length + 1.0471015 * sepal_width - 1.5503612 * petal_length - 2.1058154 * petal_width + 3.525434)  # layer 1 neuron 5
    h1_6 = sigmoid(0.032186575 * sepal_length + 1.6979355 * sepal_width - 1.9070662 * petal_length - 2.1982808 * petal_width + 1.2214696)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = sigmoid(0.7967477 * sepal_length + 1.0553067 * sepal_width - 1.6056373 * petal_length - 2.9879522 * petal_width + 4.2268558)  # layer 1 neuron 7
    o0 = -2.057382 * h1_0 - 1.7032543 * h1_1 - 3.0180497 * h1_2 - 1.0537422 * h1_3 - 1.8192966 * h1_4 + 2.4454315 * h1_5 + 2.242603 * h1_6 + 4.3464246 * h1_7 + 1.7395794  # output 0
    o1 = 1.8746146 * h1_0 - 1.8982844 * h1_1 + 2.2332869 * h1_2 - 3.2793095 * h1_3 + 1.3336766 * h1_4 + 2.7835011 * h1_5 - 4.5246153 * h1_6 + 3.9423933 * h1_7 - 0.4874488  # output 1
    o2 = 0.7420001 * h1_0 + 2.6454365 * h1_1 + 0.9988844 * h1_2 + 3.4747553 * h1_3 + 1.398811 * h1_4 - 3.9798114 * h1_5 - 0.93066216 * h1_6 - 4.970314 * h1_7 - 0.19760963  # output 2
```

```python
def net(sepal_length, sepal_width, petal_length, petal_width):
    # class 0: petal_length and petal_width small relative to sepal_width (sepal_length has a tiny negative weight)
    small = sepal_width - petal_length - 1.1 * petal_width - 0.025 * sepal_length + 0.55
    # class 1 vs 2: a second, steeper cut in the same direction (sepal_length and sepal_width push to 1)
    mid = 0.6 * sepal_length + sepal_width - 1.5 * petal_length - 2.5 * petal_width + 4.85
    if small > 0:
        return 0
    elif mid > 0:
        return 1
    else:
        return 2
```

AST tool (no LLM, `ast3`):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: linear-scores)
def net(sepal_length, sepal_width, petal_length, petal_width):
    s1 = 0.018860756779682925 * sepal_length - 0.6122898262832656 * sepal_width + 0.6085865144899568 * petal_length + 0.6820753889538306 * petal_width - 0.35662539728513326
    s2 = -0.059071078829035076 * sepal_length - 0.7431849497749798 * sepal_width + 0.7996546936475558 * petal_length + petal_width - 0.9533116026221559
    return argmax(0, s1, s2)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does
4 float inputs, 8 sigmoid hidden units, 3-class argmax. All 8 hidden units look along nearly
the same direction: about `-x1 + x2 + x3` (x2 and x3 positive, x1 negative, x0 small). Units 5-7 are
the same direction with the opposite sign. So the net is close to a one-dimensional ordinal classifier:
small values of (x2 + x3 - x1) give class 0, middle values give class 1, large values give class 2. x0 and x1
change the slope a bit between the two cuts.

No confident point has class 0 and class 2 as its top two outputs, so the classes form a chain
0 | 1 | 2, and two cuts are enough: "0 vs the rest" and then "1 vs 2".

#### What I did
1. Sampled the checker's own input set (uniform + corners + boundary points) and computed the net's
   three outputs.
2. For each pair (0-1, 1-2), fitted a linear model of `o_a - o_b` with least squares, using only points
   where |o_a - o_b| < 1..4 (near the boundary). The fits were stable across window sizes. The plain
   fitted tree `0 if L01 > 0 elif L12 > 0: 1 else 2` already had 0 disagreements outside the unsure
   zone, so the sigmoids' curvature does not matter at this tolerance.
3. Snapped the coefficients with a vectorised grid search per boundary. For each candidate I counted
   confident points (outside the unsure zone) that land on the wrong side, using only points near the
   fitted boundary. I tried several normalisations (x2 coefficient = 1, x0 = 1, x3 = 2.5) and kept
   the one with the simplest numbers and 0 errors:
   - 0 vs rest: `x1 - x2 - 1.1*x3 - x0/40 + 0.55 > 0`. Dropping the x0 term costs about 13 errors at best,
     so the small x0 weight is real. The feasible bias range for this form is about 0.51-0.56.
   - 1 vs 2: `0.6*x0 + x1 - 1.5*x2 - 2.5*x3 + 4.85 > 0`. This is tight: bias 4.8 or 4.9 already gives
     20-30 confident errors.
4. Result: `PASS (snap)`, 0 confident disagreements. Checker seeds 1-5 (fresh random sets) also gave 0.
   `PASS (exact)` would need the full sigmoid net, so I did not try for it.

The inputs' ranges look like Iris measurements (x0, x1 sepal length and width; x2, x3 petal length
and width). Class 0 is "small x2/x3", class 2 is "large x2/x3", but I kept the neutral names.

#### Heuristics
- When all first-layer rows of a small net point in nearly the same direction (up to sign), the net is
  an ordinal 1-D classifier. Look for a chain of classes and write it as an if/elif chain of linear cuts,
  not as an argmax.
- Check which class pairs are actually adjacent. Count points where (top1, top2) is each pair near
  the decision boundary. Pairs that never meet need no rule.
- Get each boundary by least-squares fitting the output difference `o_a - o_b` on points near zero
  (|diff| < a few units), not over the whole domain. Saturated sigmoids far away spoil a global fit.
- Snap per boundary, not jointly. Restrict the evaluation to confident points near the fitted boundary,
  and evaluate all candidates at once with a matrix product. A naive full-set loop over a 5-D grid is
  far too slow.
- Try several normalisations (divide by different coefficients) before snapping. One of them often
  gives much rounder numbers (here x3 = 2.5, x2 = 1.5 gave 0.6 / 1 / 1.5 / 2.5).
- The unsure-zone width divided by the gradient of the score difference gives how precisely each
  coefficient must be snapped. For sigmoid argmax nets with steep outputs, this allows only about
  0.01-0.05 of slack, so "nice" values may need two decimals.
- Re-run the checker logic with other random seeds to make sure the snapped rule did not overfit one
  input set.

</details>

```python
def net(sepal_length, sepal_width, petal_length, petal_width):
    h1_0 = sigmoid(0.57448363 * sepal_length + 0.9807926 * sepal_width - 1.3001645 * petal_length - 2.4984899 * petal_width + 3.4036396)  # layer 1 neuron 0
    h1_1 = sigmoid(0.48616055 * sepal_length - 2.1785817 * sepal_width + 1.0872575 * petal_length + 1.1529241 * petal_width - 2.2736852)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    h1_2 = sigmoid(-0.4338628 * sepal_length - 0.5395803 * sepal_width + 0.92274773 * petal_length + 2.396508 * petal_width - 4.0992165)  # layer 1 neuron 2
    h1_3 = sigmoid(0.44984555 * sepal_length + 1.4469069 * sepal_width - 2.372314 * petal_length - 2.266382 * petal_width + 1.0780796)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    h1_4 = sigmoid(-0.36993712 * sepal_length + 2.2686222 * sepal_width - 1.8520628 * petal_length - 2.107014 * petal_width + 1.260194)  # layer 1 neuron 4
    h1_5 = sigmoid(0.05775549 * sepal_length - 1.6215857 * sepal_width + 1.7806549 * petal_length + 0.9623498 * petal_width - 1.5379227)  # layer 1 neuron 5
    h1_6 = sigmoid(0.665789 * sepal_length + 1.0026428 * sepal_width - 1.5921929 * petal_length - 2.2401886 * petal_width + 3.4889195)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = sigmoid(0.6278978 * sepal_length + 0.94514346 * sepal_width - 1.595281 * petal_length - 2.0652645 * petal_width + 3.6681252)  # layer 1 neuron 7
    o0 = 1.780619 * h1_0 - 1.9650717 * h1_1 - 1.5517404 * h1_2 + 3.562395 * h1_3 + 3.3714907 * h1_4 - 2.23642 * h1_5 + 1.5555508 * h1_6 + 1.3977468 * h1_7 - 0.95048356  # output 0
    o1 = 3.8749874 * h1_0 + 0.6679351 * h1_1 - 2.7578254 * h1_2 - 6.042837 * h1_3 - 3.5092783 * h1_4 + 1.3809556 * h1_5 + 4.073249 * h1_6 + 3.9619339 * h1_7 - 1.1659423  # output 1
    o2 = -4.151219 * h1_0 + 2.38604 * h1_1 + 3.0881505 * h1_2 - 1.8208975 * h1_3 - 0.6432301 * h1_4 + 1.7288395 * h1_5 - 4.499098 * h1_6 - 4.0601482 * h1_7 + 1.0030161  # output 2
```

```python
def net(sepal_length, sepal_width, petal_length, petal_width):
    # The net's three argmax regions are cut by two planes (each found by a max-margin fit
    # on the net's confident decisions; no rounder coefficients keep a positive margin).
    # Class 0: petal_length and petal_width small compared with sepal_width.
    first_class_score = petal_length + 0.94 * petal_width - 0.88 * sepal_width - 0.02 * sepal_length
    if first_class_score <= 0.5:
        return 0
    # Class 2 vs class 1: petal_width (and petal_length) large compared with sepal_width and sepal_length.
    # (this plane has the thinnest margin, about 0.03; 2-decimal coefficients flip a corner point)
    last_class_score = petal_width + 0.597 * petal_length - 0.412 * sepal_width - 0.236 * sepal_length
    if last_class_score > 1.953:
        return 2
    return 1
```

```python
    s1 = -0.015118026750453208 * sepal_length - 0.6044661142518187 * sepal_width + 0.6965374727240982 * petal_length + 0.6549651996734531 * petal_width - 0.3675800228039594
    s2 = -0.09664182206055612 * sepal_length - 0.7466325493695645 * sepal_width + 0.9020796386891347 * petal_length + petal_width - 1.0360489046664287
    return argmax(0, s1, s2)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the dump is

4 float inputs -> 8 sigmoid units -> 3 outputs, argmax. The hidden units fall into four
correlated groups (corr > 0.9 on the input box):
- A = h0, h6, h7, roughly sigmoid(0.6 x0 + x1 - 1.5 x2 - 2.25 x3 + 3.5);
- B = h3, h4, which mainly drive class 0;
- C = h1, h5;
- D = h2 (x3 large).

The class differences are then o1 - o2 ~ 25 A - 7 B - ..., o0 - o1 ~ 16 B - 7 A - 6 C, so
the net works like a decision list: class 0 when B is on, otherwise class 1 when A is on,
otherwise class 2.

#### What I did

1. Sampled the checker's own input set (seed 0) and labelled it with the net. "Confident" means
   outside the unsure zone (top-2 gap >= 0.515).
2. Fitted a logistic regression for each class pair on the confident points. All three pairs
   were linearly separable with no errors. So the 8 sigmoids reduce to two planes, used as a
   decision list:
   - `x2 + 0.94 x3 - 0.88 x1 - 0.02 x0 <= 0.5` gives class 0;
   - otherwise `x3 + 0.597 x2 - 0.412 x1 - 0.236 x0 > 1.953` gives class 2;
   - otherwise class 1.
3. Snapping. For a fixed direction w, the feasible thresholds are the interval
   [max w.x over the negative class, min w.x over the positive class]. Its width (the "gap") is
   concave in w. I maximised it with coordinate and random search, then tried rounder w:
   - Plane 0|12: max gap 0.068. 2-decimal coefficients keep a gap of 0.060, and 0.5 lies inside
     the feasible interval. Dropping x0 (gap 0.013) or using 1/0.9/0.875-style values (negative gap)
     fails.
   - Plane 1|2: max gap only 0.033. 2-decimal coefficients (0.6, 0.42, 0.24, threshold 1.92)
     pass seed 0 but fail one confident point on seed 5. That point is in a box corner (x1 low,
     x2 high, x3 low), where the net's real boundary bends. I kept the 3-decimal max-margin plane.
     No nicer forms (1/4, 2/5, 3/5, 2/3, ...) separate the classes.
4. Checked the result with `check.py` (PASS snap, 0 confident disagreements). I also ran
   `check_program` with seeds 1-5, and all passed with 0 confident disagreements.

The result is not exact: about 4k inputs in the unsure zone disagree. The sigmoid boundaries
are slightly curved, and a plane cannot follow them inside the zone.

#### Heuristics

- For argmax/binary nets, first label the checker's input set with the net, keep only the
  confident points, and test whether each class pair is linearly separable in the raw inputs.
  Sigmoid/tanh nets with few hidden units often are, even when the hidden layer looks
  complicated. In that case the whole net becomes 1-2 linear comparisons in a decision list
  (one class vs rest first, then the remaining pair).
- Correlated hidden units (|corr| > 0.9 over the input box) are copies of one feature. Summing
  their output weights per group shows the decision-list order (which class each group turns on).
- To snap a separating plane, keep the direction separate from the threshold. For each direction,
  the allowed thresholds form an interval [max over the negatives, min over the positives].
  Its width is concave in the direction, so local search finds the max-margin direction.
  Then round the direction as far as the interval stays non-empty, and pick a round threshold
  inside it.
- Logistic regression is a good starting point, but it is not max-margin. A plane with a tiny
  margin passes one input set and fails on another seed. Re-check with a few other
  `check_program` seeds before trusting a snapped plane. Failures show up in box corners, far
  from the training data, where the net's boundary curves.
- When the margin is thin (gap < ~0.05 in input units), prefer 3-decimal coefficients over a
  "nicer" but fragile plane.

</details>

```python
    o2 = -0.7232819 * h1_0 + 1.3536601 * h1_1 - 1.028976 * h1_2 + 3.440322 * h1_3 - 3.8369398 * h1_4 - 4.964316 * h1_5 - 0.088349685 * h1_6 + 3.3370752 * h1_7 + 0.36445343  # output 2
```

```python
# Three classes separated by two straight cuts (the net's 8 sigmoid units fold into these).
# Both cuts weigh the inputs the same way: sepal_width and sepal_length push up, petal_length and petal_width push down.
def net(sepal_length, sepal_width, petal_length, petal_width):
    # class 0: small petal_length + petal_width compared with sepal_width (and a little sepal_length)
    low_cut = sepal_length + 6 * sepal_width + 3.1 - 8 * (petal_length + petal_width)
    # class 2: large petal_length and petal_width (petal_width counts 1.65x) compared with sepal_length and sepal_width
    high_cut = 20 * petal_length + 33 * petal_width - 8 * sepal_length - 14 * sepal_width - 63
    if low_cut > 0:
        return 0
    if high_cut > 0:
        return 2
    return 1
```

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: linear-scores)
def net(sepal_length, sepal_width, petal_length, petal_width):
    s1 = -0.07579271252283547 * sepal_length - 0.4649813488757974 * sepal_width + 0.6083749456778932 * petal_length + 0.6271940794104475 * petal_width - 0.22842107375895934
    s2 = -0.16696925768844664 * sepal_length - 0.6225118144913315 * sepal_width + 0.8348143886000494 * petal_length + petal_width - 0.9355503801687733
    return argmax(0, s1, s2)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does

4 inputs -> 8 sigmoid units -> 3 scores -> argmax. Over the whole input box the net assigns
class 1 to about 60% of points and classes 0 and 2 to about 20% each.

- Unit h1_6 has small weights, and over the box its pre-activation stays between 4.5 and 10.5, so
  sigmoid(h1_6) is between 0.989 and 1. It is a constant and folds into the output biases.
- The other 7 units all point roughly the same way: +x1 (and a little +x0) against -x2 and -x3.
  They come in sign-flipped pairs (h1_0 and h1_1, for example), so the net is close to sorting
  points along a single direction, with two cut points.
- I fitted a linear separator for each class against the rest on the points where the net is
  confident (the checker's own input set, outside the unsure zone). Both were linearly separable,
  but only just: the largest margin was about 0.03 to 0.04 in units of x2.
- To snap the coefficients I used an LP that maximises the margin with some coefficients fixed to
  round values. I kept a snapped choice only while the margin stayed positive.

#### Result (`agent.py`)

```
class 0  if  x0 + 6*x1 + 3.1 > 8*(x2 + x3)
class 2  elif 20*x2 + 33*x3 > 8*x0 + 14*x1 + 63
class 1  otherwise
```

- The class-0 cut in normalised form is x2 + x3 < x0/8 + 3/4*x1 + 0.3875.
- The class-2 cut in normalised form is x2 + 1.65*x3 > 0.4*x0 + 0.7*x1 + 3.15.
- The whole network is replaced by two linear inequalities with integer coefficients. There are
  no sigmoids and no hidden units.
- `check.py`: PASS (snap). There are 0 disagreements where the net is confident; the 4573
  disagreements are all in the unsure zone, which covers 2.8% of inputs. With the checker's input
  set re-drawn from seeds 1 to 5 it also passes, with 0 disagreements outside the zone each time.
- It is not exact. The net's boundaries are slightly curved sigmoid surfaces, so no flat cut
  reproduces them exactly.
- Coefficients I could not snap further:
  - Setting the x3 weight of the class-0 cut to exactly 1 still works.
  - x0/10 instead of x0/8 fails.
  - Dropping x0 from either cut fails, so x0 really matters, a little.
  - In the class-2 cut, the x3 weight has to stay between about 1.6 and 1.67.

#### Heuristics

- **Sigmoid units with a pre-activation range far from 0 are constants.** Compute the range of
  each pre-activation over the input box. If it stays above about 4.5 (or below -4.5), replace
  the unit by 1 (or 0).
- **Check whether the hidden units share one direction.** If most units have the same sign pattern
  in their input weights, possibly negated, a softmax/argmax net is effectively ordinal along a
  projection. Try one linear cut per extreme class before anything structural.
- **Find each class against the rest with a max-margin LP on the confident points only.** Points
  in the unsure zone may disagree, so leave them out of the fit. A positive LP margin proves that
  a linear rule can pass.
- **Snap by fixing coefficients in the LP one at a time.** Fix a coefficient to a round value,
  re-solve for the rest, and keep it only while the margin stays positive. Then scale the whole
  inequality to clear the denominators and get integer coefficients.
- **Avoid brute-force grid searches over coefficients on 200k+ points.** They are slow, and a
  coarse grid misses thin feasible regions. The LP finds the feasible region directly.
- **Write the cascade in order: extreme class 0, then extreme class 2, then the middle class.**
  Each one-vs-rest cut is valid on its own, so the order of the `if`s does not create errors.
- **Re-check with other input-set seeds when margins are thin.** This confirms that the pass is
  not luck from one sample.

</details>

```python
    h1_1 = sigmoid(0.8473696 * sepal_length + 1.0622777 * sepal_width - 1.9054812 * petal_length - 2.4356616 * petal_width + 4.127838)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    h1_2 = sigmoid(-0.74788326 * sepal_length - 0.6769546 * sepal_width + 1.4498872 * petal_length + 2.340841 * petal_width - 3.355143)  # layer 1 neuron 2
    h1_3 = sigmoid(-0.73994744 * sepal_length - 1.0490681 * sepal_width + 1.6337281 * petal_length + 2.4280596 * petal_width - 3.5028436)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    h1_4 = sigmoid(0.21110485 * sepal_length + 1.4139358 * sepal_width - 2.0891743 * petal_length - 1.3273305 * petal_width + 1.068982)  # layer 1 neuron 4
    h1_5 = sigmoid(0.54325354 * sepal_length + 1.2152239 * sepal_width - 2.324003 * petal_length - 1.5286368 * petal_width + 0.6486973)  # layer 1 neuron 5
    h1_6 = sigmoid(0.030278841 * sepal_length - 1.7344655 * sepal_width + 1.8130618 * petal_length + 1.6683291 * petal_width - 1.0934159)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = sigmoid(0.4567453 * sepal_length + 1.2863278 * sepal_width - 1.3948557 * petal_length - 2.7504666 * petal_width + 4.624438)  # layer 1 neuron 7
    o0 = -1.8089625 * h1_0 + 1.9734794 * h1_1 - 2.58092 * h1_2 - 1.4844991 * h1_3 + 2.8893514 * h1_4 + 2.8872352 * h1_5 - 1.7734259 * h1_6 + 1.7824466 * h1_7 - 0.012949362  # output 0
    o1 = 2.2875223 * h1_0 + 3.477093 * h1_1 - 1.8549018 * h1_2 - 2.62296 * h1_3 - 2.7559412 * h1_4 - 4.7999835 * h1_5 + 0.6215765 * h1_6 + 4.1019125 * h1_7 + 0.29027295  # output 1
    o2 = 0.91799027 * h1_0 - 3.9715836 * h1_1 + 3.11079 * h1_2 + 3.3854237 * h1_3 - 1.0468202 * h1_4 - 1.3433133 * h1_5 + 0.3342132 * h1_6 - 4.1917067 * h1_7 + 0.078377254  # output 2
```

```python
def net(sepal_length, sepal_width, petal_length, petal_width):
    # Two (almost exactly) planar decision boundaries; class 1 is the slab between them.
    low_score = petal_length + 0.75 * petal_width - 0.66 * sepal_width - 0.14 * sepal_length     # boundary class 0 | class 1
    high_score = petal_width + 0.62 * petal_length - 0.42 * sepal_width - 0.27 * sepal_length    # boundary class 1 | class 2
    if low_score < 0.4:
        return 0
    if high_score > 1.8:
        return 2
    return 1
```

AST tool (no LLM, `ast3`):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: linear-scores)
def net(sepal_length, sepal_width, petal_length, petal_width):
    s1 = -0.1106026473744685 * sepal_length - 0.5282471463662933 * sepal_width + 0.7999913224910224 * petal_length + 0.5987669029079844 * petal_width - 0.3143575306149774
    s2 = -0.19487170336520193 * sepal_length - 0.6637289937604786 * sepal_width + petal_length + 0.9189090880090388 * petal_width - 0.9058535830940236
    return argmax(0, s1, s2)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does
- 4 inputs -> 8 sigmoid units -> 3 linear outputs, argmax.
- The 8 hidden pre-activations are almost collinear (|corr| 0.91-1.00 over the box): they all
  measure roughly one direction, "+x2 +x3 -x1 (-x0 a little)". Units come in sign-flipped pairs
  (h0/h6 vs h4/h5, h2/h3 vs h1/h7).
- Over the box only two class pairs ever compete: 0|1 and 1|2. Classes 0 and 2 never touch.
  So the classes are ordered along that direction: class 0 low, class 1 a middle slab, class 2 high.
- I bisected about 2500 points onto each decision boundary (where the two top outputs are equal)
  and ran an SVD on them. The smallest singular value was 0.001-0.002 (vs 0.5-0.9 for the
  other directions), so both boundaries are planes to within about 0.002 on average and at most
  0.025 in input units. The sigmoids bend them only a little.

#### Program
    low_score  = x2 + 0.75*x3 - 0.66*x1 - 0.14*x0   ; class 0 if low_score  < 0.4
    high_score = x3 + 0.62*x2 - 0.42*x1 - 0.27*x0   ; class 2 if high_score > 1.8
    otherwise class 1

- Plane fits (leading coefficient fixed at 1): 0|1 x2 = 0.138 x0 + 0.661 x1 - 0.749 x3 + 0.393;
  1|2 x3 = 0.263 x0 + 0.424 x1 - 0.625 x2 + 1.846.
- Snapping: the unsure zone is narrow (score < 0.49 with outputs spanning about 10), so the
  coefficients can't go much past 2 decimals. Simpler fractions failed: 1/8, 2/3 or 0.15 for
  the 0|1 plane, and 0.25 with 0.4 for the 1|2 plane. I searched a 0.01 grid around each fit for
  zero confident disagreements and kept the candidate with the fewest decimal digits. Integer
  versions also exist, but they need scale 15-19 (15*x2 + 11*x3 - 10*x1 - 2*x0 < 6;
  19*x3 + 12*x2 - 8*x1 - 5*x0 > 36), so they aren't more readable.
- Result: PASS (snap), with 0 confident disagreements on the checker set and on 4 more input
  sets (seeds 11-14, about 243k points each). PASS (exact) isn't reachable with planes, because
  the true boundaries are slightly curved sigmoid level sets.
- If the inputs are the classic 4 flower measurements (in the order sepal length, sepal width,
  petal length, petal width), then the rule reads "petal size beats sepal width". That is only a
  guess from the ranges and is not used anywhere.

#### Heuristics
- For argmax nets, first find which class pairs are actually adjacent. Look at the top-2 pair
  where the gap is small. Non-adjacent pairs need no boundary in the program. If the adjacent pairs
  form a chain (0|1, 1|2), the program is an ordered if-chain with one plane per link.
- Fit boundaries directly instead of the outputs. Bisect random segments whose ends have different
  classes onto the boundary, then SVD the points. A tiny last singular value means the boundary is a
  plane, whatever the activation function, and the singular vector gives the coefficients.
  Fitting the output logits linearly gave poor R^2 (0.6) even though the decision boundaries
  were near-perfect planes.
- Strongly correlated hidden pre-activations (|corr| > 0.9) suggest the net is mostly one-dimensional,
  and its smooth sigmoid boundaries are close to planes.
- Normalise each plane so the dominant input has coefficient 1. Then snap the other coefficients
  and the bias by a grid search that minimises "decimal digits used" subject to zero confident
  disagreements. The unsure-zone width decides how far you can snap. Check the result on a few
  input sets with other seeds before trusting it.
- Integer-coefficient planes only appear at large scales (15-20) when the margin is tight. Prefer
  2-decimal coefficients in that case.
- Separate class-ordering checks (`if low < t0: return 0`, `if high > t1: return 2`) are safe
  when the two boundaries don't cross inside the box. Check that no confident class-0 point lies
  above the second plane.

</details>

```python
    h1_4 = sigmoid(-0.876331 * petal_length - 0.7369177 * petal_width + 5.4177823)  # layer 1 neuron 4
    h1_5 = sigmoid(-0.8293763 * petal_length - 1.0147086 * petal_width + 5.678925)  # layer 1 neuron 5
    h1_6 = sigmoid(-0.7421966 * petal_length - 0.96439767 * petal_width + 4.7099404)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = sigmoid(-1.3849044 * petal_length - 2.5346978 * petal_width + 4.9175243)  # layer 1 neuron 7

    o2 = 4.425873 * h1_0 - 1.8535136 * h1_1 - 1.6078502 * h1_2 + 3.2966425 * h1_3 - 4.4366326 * h1_4 - 3.9088402 * h1_5 - 4.450044 * h1_6 - 1.1066092 * h1_7 + 1.8857558  # output 2
```

```python
def net(petal_length, petal_width):
    # Three classes ordered along the direction "petal_length plus a multiple of petal_width".
    # Each class boundary is a straight line in the (petal_length, petal_width) plane.
    low_score = petal_length + 7 / 3 * petal_width      # class 0 / 1 boundary: low_score = 4
    high_score = petal_length + 9 / 8 * petal_width     # class 1 / 2 boundary: high_score = 6.875
    if low_score < 4:
        return 0
    if high_score < 6.875:
        return 1
    return 2
```

AST tool (no LLM, `ast3`):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: linear-scores)
def net(petal_length, petal_width):
    s1 = 0.26 * petal_length + 0.61 * petal_width - 1.05
    s2 = 0.61 * petal_length + petal_width - 3.43
    return argmax(0, s1, s2)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does

2 inputs -> 8 sigmoid units -> 3 logits, argmax. Mapping the argmax on a grid over the domain
(x0 in [1, 6.9], x1 in [0.1, 2.5]) shows three bands ordered along a "x0 plus some x1" direction:
class 0 at small x0/x1, class 1 in the middle, class 2 at large values. Classes 0 and 2 never touch
inside the domain.

Bisecting the class-change points on 49 horizontal lines and fitting a line to each boundary:

- 0/1 boundary: x0 = -2.3343 * x1 + 4.0136 (max deviation from the line 0.006)
- 1/2 boundary: x0 = -1.1236 * x1 + 6.8660 (max deviation 0.009)

So, despite 8 sigmoid hidden units, both boundaries are straight lines to within 0.01.

#### What I changed

The whole net is replaced by two linear tests (a decision list):

    if x0 + 7/3 * x1 < 4:      return 0
    if x0 + 9/8 * x1 < 6.875:  return 1
    return 2

Snapped values: 2.3343 -> 7/3, 4.0136 -> 4, 1.1236 -> 9/8, 6.866 -> 6.875 (= 55/8).
Result: PASS (snap), 0 disagreements where the net is confident; all 3585 differences are in the
net's unsure zone. The raw dump itself gets PASS (exact) but still differs in 574 unsure-zone points
(float noise), so an exact pass is not reachable with anything simpler than the dump.

Simpler snaps fail: x0 + 2*x1 < 3.8, x0 + 2.5*x1 < 4.1, x0 + x1 < 6.6 all give confident
disagreements (the slopes matter to about 0.01-0.05).

#### Heuristics

- For low-dimensional argmax nets, first print the argmax on a coarse grid. That shows how many
  regions there are, which classes are adjacent, and whether the boundaries look straight.
- Find boundary points by bisection along one input for many values of the other, then
  least-squares fit `x0 = a*x1 + c` per adjacent class pair. If the max residual is small compared
  with the domain, the boundary is a line, whatever the hidden activation (sigmoid units included).
- When classes are ordered along one direction (0 | 1 | 2 bands), write the result as a decision
  list: test the lowest boundary first, then the next. Non-adjacent class pairs then need no test.
- Snap slopes to simple fractions (7/3, 9/8) rather than forcing integer slopes; argmax checks
  tolerate errors only inside the unsure zone, and slope errors grow across the whole domain, so
  check a coarse snap and step back to a finer fraction if it fails.
- Snap the intercept after the slope: refit or reread the intercept with the snapped slope before
  rounding it.
- Do not chase PASS (exact) on sigmoid nets: even the original dump differs from the reference in
  the unsure zone, so the snap pass is the realistic target.

</details>

```python
    o2 = 3.9806092 * h1_0 - 3.9905887 * h1_1 - 0.9188162 * h1_2 - 3.7627861 * h1_3 + 2.1525452 * h1_4 + 5.024216 * h1_5 - 3.7207181 * h1_6 - 3.1355655 * h1_7 + 0.043042753  # output 2
```

```python
# Three classes ordered along a diagonal of the (petal_length, petal_width) plane, split by two straight lines.
# Class 0: small petal_length and petal_width; class 1: middle band; class 2: large petal_length and petal_width.
def net(petal_length, petal_width):
    low_score = petal_length + 2.2 * petal_width      # boundary class 0 | class 1 at 4
    high_score = petal_length + 1.1 * petal_width     # boundary class 1 | class 2 at 6.8
    if low_score < 4:
        return 0
    if high_score < 6.8:
        return 1
    return 2
```

AST tool (no LLM, `ast3`):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: linear-scores)
def net(petal_length, petal_width):
    s1 = 0.27626143577568874 * petal_length + 0.5909463553345534 * petal_width - 1.0991641331616555
    s2 = 0.6405733940315114 * petal_length + petal_width - 3.5763701619959662
    return argmax(0, s1, s2)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does
2 inputs -> 8 sigmoid units -> 3 logits -> argmax. Mapping the argmax on a grid showed three
regions in diagonal bands: class 0 at small x0/x1, class 1 in the middle, class 2 at large x0/x1.
Both class boundaries are almost straight lines (bisected at 13 values of x1 each):

- 0|1 boundary: x0 = 3.97 - 2.14*x1 (crosses x0 = 1 near x1 = 1.39, so above that x1 there is no class 0)
- 1|2 boundary: x0 = 6.81 - 1.12*x1 (very slight curvature, < 0.03 in x0)

The unsure zone (top-two logit margin < 0.43) is only about +-0.07 wide in x0 around each
boundary, so the snapped lines had to stay inside that band over the whole x1 range.

#### What I changed
Replaced the 8 sigmoids and 3 logit sums by two linear tests:

    if x0 + 2.2*x1 < 4:   class 0
    elif x0 + 1.1*x1 < 6.8: class 1
    else: class 2

Coefficients tried: slope 2 or 1 (too far from 2.14 / 1.12, drifts outside the band at the ends
of the x1 range), then 2.2 / 1.1 with round intercepts 4 / 6.8, which pass. 2.125 / 1.125 also
pass with fewer disagreements, but 2.2 and 1.1 are simpler (and 2.2 = 2 * 1.1).
The rule order is safe: whenever x0 + 2.2*x1 < 4, also x0 + 1.1*x1 < 4 < 6.8.
Result: PASS (snap), 0 disagreements where the net is confident. An exact version would need the
full sigmoid mixture, so I did not pursue it.

The input ranges (x0 in [1, 6.9], x1 in [0.1, 2.5]) and the three ordered classes look like a
measurement-based 3-class classification with two linear cuts in a 2-D feature plane.

#### Heuristics
- For small argmax nets with 2 inputs, first print the argmax on a coarse grid; the region shape
  (bands, wedges, islands) tells you the program structure before reading any weights.
- Bisect each class boundary along one input at many values of the other and fit a line; if the
  residual is small, a single linear test per boundary replaces the whole hidden layer.
- Compute the unsure-zone band (where top-two logit margin < threshold) along each boundary in
  input units; it is the real budget for snapping. Choose the simplest coefficient whose line stays
  inside the band at both ends of the input range (ends are where slope errors show up).
- Write the boundary as `x0 + k*x1 < c` (normalising the coefficient of one input to 1) so k and c
  can be snapped independently to short decimals.
- For ordered classes, a cascade `if s1 < a: 0 elif s2 < b: 1 else: 2` replaces argmax; check that
  the first test implies the second so the cascade cannot skip a class inconsistently.
- Sigmoid nets give smooth, nearly linear boundaries when the units are spread out; their curvature
  is usually far below the unsure-zone width, so piecewise rules are not needed.

</details>
