```python
# dumped from a neural network: 2 inputs -> 8 -> 3 outputs
# inputs: petal_length: float [1, 6.9], petal_width: float [0.1, 2.5]
# output: argmax over 3 outputs: the returned class index
def net(petal_length, petal_width):
    h1_0 = sigmoid(-0.84715724 * petal_length - 0.8084469 * petal_width + 5.4324207)  # layer 1 neuron 0
    h1_1 = sigmoid(-0.74336237 * petal_length - 0.7039953 * petal_width + 4.520175)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    h1_2 = sigmoid(0.8109278 * petal_length + 3.7180254 * petal_width - 4.6540127)  # layer 1 neuron 2
    h1_3 = sigmoid(-1.1383586 * petal_length - 3.3893106 * petal_width + 5.1617327)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    h1_4 = sigmoid(1.680434 * petal_length + 2.552029 * petal_width - 6.041179)  # layer 1 neuron 4
    h1_5 = sigmoid(0.8267112 * petal_length + 0.82492334 * petal_width - 5.3719106)  # layer 1 neuron 5
    h1_6 = sigmoid(0.70741904 * petal_length + 0.9668199 * petal_width - 4.8473077)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = sigmoid(0.75871783 * petal_length + 1.0459445 * petal_width - 5.413136)  # layer 1 neuron 7
    o0 = 2.4306648 * h1_0 + 3.0377429 * h1_1 - 2.9805567 * h1_2 + 2.5517929 * h1_3 - 3.831646 * h1_4 - 2.169625 * h1_5 - 2.3304102 * h1_6 - 1.6322078 * h1_7 + 1.6498932  # output 0
    o1 = 3.064072 * h1_0 + 2.9105508 * h1_1 + 1.9104679 * h1_2 - 5.3547773 * h1_3 + 2.779952 * h1_4 - 2.9832118 * h1_5 - 2.5431976 * h1_6 - 3.2075133 * h1_7 - 0.6533506  # output 1
    o2 = -4.1293664 * h1_0 - 4.537461 * h1_1 + 1.0265776 * h1_2 - 1.2210994 * h1_3 + 0.2721263 * h1_4 + 4.063673 * h1_5 + 3.7486837 * h1_6 + 3.4156926 * h1_7 - 0.8782417  # output 2
    return argmax(o0, o1, o2)
```

```python
# Two-input, three-class classifier. The 8 sigmoid units only carve two
# almost straight cuts through the (petal_length, petal_width) plane:
#   class 0  below  petal_length + 2.5*petal_width = 4.3   (small petal_length and small petal_width)
#   class 2  above  petal_length + 1.1*petal_width = 6.7   (large petal_length and/or large petal_width)
#   class 1  in the band between them.
# The two lines never meet inside the input box, so the regions are ordered.
def net(petal_length, petal_width):
    low_score = petal_length + 2.5 * petal_width
    high_score = petal_length + 1.1 * petal_width
    if low_score < 4.3:
        return 0
    if high_score < 6.7:
        return 1
    return 2
```

AST tool (no LLM, `ast3`):

```python
    s2 = 0.6116726519178519 * petal_length + petal_width - 3.5794586751235533
    return argmax(0, s1, s2)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What I did

- The dump is a 2 -> 8 sigmoid -> 3 argmax net. Instead of simplifying the units one by one,
  I printed the class map on a 60x25 grid over the input box. It showed three bands separated by
  two nearly straight lines: class 0 in the low-x0/low-x1 corner, class 2 in the high corner,
  class 1 in between.
- I bisected each boundary in x0 at 49 values of x1 and fitted `x0 = a*x1 + b` by least squares:
  - 0|1: `x0 = -2.619*x1 + 4.368` (max deviation 0.011, so practically a straight line);
  - 1|2: `x0 = -1.086*x1 + 6.666` (max deviation 0.036, slightly curved).
- The fitted lines pass as-is (PASS snap). Then I snapped the coefficients and tried several
  candidates against the checker:
  - `x0 + 2.5*x1 < 4.3` and `x0 + 1.1*x1 < 6.7` pass, with 0 disagreements where the net is
    confident;
  - `x0 + x1 < 6.5 / 6.55 / 6.6` fails at the high-x1 end (the 1|2 slope really is about 1.1);
  - `x0 + 3*x1 < 4.5` fails (the 0|1 slope is about 2.6, not 3).
- Final program: two linear thresholds checked in order (0, then 1, else 2). The lines do not
  cross inside the box, so an if-chain is equivalent to the 3-way argmax. Result: PASS (snap).
  All disagreements (1.8% of the test inputs) are in the net's unsure zone. It cannot be exact,
  because the sigmoid boundaries are slightly curved.

#### Heuristics

- For low-dimensional argmax nets (2 inputs), first print the decision map on a coarse grid
  instead of reading weights. Smooth sigmoid nets with many units often just draw a few
  straight cuts.
- To find a boundary, bisect along one input at many values of the other, then fit a line.
  The maximum residual tells you whether one linear threshold is enough.
- If the class regions are ordered bands (class k sits between k-1 and k+1) and the boundaries
  do not cross inside the input box, write the argmax as an if-chain of threshold tests.
- Snap the slope first (to 1, 1.1, 2.5, ...), then refit the intercept for that slope. If a
  rounder slope fails, the failures show up at the far end of the boundary, away from where
  the line pivots.
- For sigmoid nets, an exact pass is usually out of reach with piecewise-linear code. Aim for
  zero disagreements where the net is confident, and accept differences in the unsure zone.
- Coefficient sizes in the hidden layer hint at the boundary slopes. Here, units 2 and 3 have
  x1/x0 weight ratios of about 3-4.6, and units 5-7 about 1-1.4. Treat this only as a hint and
  confirm it with bisection.

</details>

```python
# 3-class classifier over two inputs, decided by two straight cuts in the (petal_length, petal_width) plane.
# Class 0 = "both small" corner, class 2 = "both large" corner, class 1 = the band between.
def net(petal_length, petal_width):
    low_score = petal_length + 4.25 * petal_width      # steep cut: petal_width matters ~4x more than petal_length
    high_score = petal_length + 1.1 * petal_width      # shallow cut: petal_length and petal_width weigh about the same
    if low_score < 5.5:
        return 0
    if high_score > 6.75:
        return 2
    return 1
```

```python
    s2 = 0.4552761517773256 * petal_length + petal_width - 2.8738100963388846
    return argmax(0, s1, s2)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What I did

- The dump is a 2 -> 8 sigmoid -> 3 argmax net. Reading the weights alone is not helpful, so I
  evaluated the net on a grid over the input box and printed the class map as text.
- The map has three classes in three bands: class 0 in the low-x0 / low-x1 corner, class 2 in the
  high-x0 / high-x1 corner, class 1 between them. The two boundaries are almost straight lines and
  never meet inside the box, so the program is two cuts in a chain (no 0/2 boundary).
- I located each boundary by scanning x0 finely for each x1 (25 rows) and fit a line x0 = c - a*x1:
  - 0|1: x0 = 5.47 - 4.21*x1 (slightly curved near x1 = 1)
  - 1|2: x0 = 6.74 - 1.10*x1
- I snapped them to x0 + 4.25*x1 < 5.5 -> 0 and x0 + 1.1*x1 > 6.75 -> 2. I compared a few snapped
  variants on 400k random points by mismatch rate per boundary. For 0|1, (4.25, 5.5) gave 0.09%
  vs 0.23% for (4.2, 5.5) and 0.45% for (4, 5.4). For 1|2, (1.1, 6.75) gave 0.20% vs 1.2% for (1, 6.6).
- Result: `PASS (snap)`, 0 disagreements where the net is confident; all the disagreements are in the
  unsure zone near the slightly curved boundaries. An exact pass would need the sigmoids back,
  because the true boundaries curve a little.

#### Heuristics

- For low-dimensional argmax nets (especially sigmoid ones), print the class map as ASCII on a grid
  first; it shows the region structure (bands, corners, which classes touch) at once.
- If the class regions are ordered bands, write the program as a chain of cuts (`if s1 < t1: return 0;
  if s2 > t2: return 2; return 1`). You only need one cut per adjacent pair, not a full argmax.
- Locate each boundary with a fine 1D scan per row, fit a line, and normalise the coefficient of one
  input to 1 (x0 + a*x1 vs t). Small coefficients and thresholds are then easy to snap.
- Snap one boundary at a time and measure its mismatch rate separately on random points. Prefer the
  simplest value whose mismatch is close to the best fit. Quarter steps (4.25) can beat a nearby
  integer by several times.
- Sigmoid nets give smoothly curved boundaries, so straight cuts usually reach only a snap pass. The
  curvature is largest where several hidden units switch at once, which is where the remaining
  mismatches are.

</details>

```python
# Two parallel-ish linear cuts split the (petal_length, petal_width) plane into three bands.
# Class 0: low corner (small petal_length and petal_width); class 2: high corner; class 1 in between.
def net(petal_length, petal_width):
    low_score = 5 * petal_length + 8 * petal_width      # boundary 0|1: petal_length + 1.6*petal_width = 3.6
    high_score = 8 * petal_length + 9 * petal_width     # boundary 1|2: petal_length + 1.125*petal_width = 6.75
    if low_score < 18:

    if high_score > 54:
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Agent notes

#### What the network does

A 2 -> 8 (sigmoid) -> 3 argmax net. Mapping the argmax over a grid showed three bands separated by
two nearly straight, non-crossing boundaries running from top-left to bottom-right. Class 0 is the
low corner (small x0 and x1), class 2 is the high corner, and class 1 is the band in between. Classes 0
and 2 never touch.

#### What I changed

- I replaced the 8 sigmoid units and 3 output scores with two linear threshold tests.
- I found each boundary by bisecting along x0 for 49 values of x1 and fitting a line to the points:
  - 0|1: x0 = -1.583*x1 + 3.594. Snapped to x0 + 1.6*x1 = 3.6, which is `5*x0 + 8*x1 < 18`.
  - 1|2: x0 = -1.127*x1 + 6.770. Snapped to x0 + 1.125*x1 = 6.75, which is `8*x0 + 9*x1 > 54`.
    This boundary bends slightly (the local slope is 1.18 near x1=0 and 1.09 near x1=2.5), so a
    straight line cannot match it exactly.
- I tried coarser snaps:
  - slope 1.5 with threshold 3.5 for 0|1: FAIL;
  - slope 1 with thresholds 6.5, 6.6 or 6.7 for 1|2: FAIL;
  - slope 1.2 with threshold 6.8 for 1|2: FAIL;
  - slopes 1.1 or 1.125 with thresholds 6.75 or 6.77, together with 1.6 / 3.6: all PASS (snap).

  I kept 9/8 and 27/4, because they give integer coefficients.
- Result: `PASS (snap)`, with 0 disagreements where the net is confident. All 4180 disagreements are
  in the unsure zone (3.55% of inputs). PASS (exact) would need the sigmoid curvature, which goes
  against the goal of a simple program.

#### Heuristics

- For low-dimensional argmax nets, print the class map on a coarse grid first. The topology
  (bands, wedges, islands, which classes touch) suggests the program's shape before any algebra.
- When classes form ordered bands, write a chain of threshold tests in that order
  (`if s0 < t0: 0 elif s1 > t1: 2 else 1`). Only adjacent classes need a boundary.
- Recover each boundary directly: bisect along one input at many values of the other, then fit a
  line. This is more reliable than reading slopes off the hidden-unit weights. Here several hidden
  units were almost parallel and combined into each boundary.
- Snap the slope first and the intercept second. Then clear denominators to get integer
  coefficients (x0 + 1.6*x1 < 3.6 becomes 5*x0 + 8*x1 < 18).
- Sigmoid nets give slightly curved boundaries. A straight line usually stays inside the unsure
  zone, but check the ends of the input range, where the curvature errors add up (failures showed
  up at the corners x1 ~ 0.1 and x1 ~ 2.5).
- Try a few candidate snaps against the checker in one loop. Keep the simplest one that passes,
  and use the number of unsure-zone disagreements to break ties.

</details>

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does
2 float inputs -> 8 sigmoid units -> 3 scores -> argmax. Mapping the argmax on a grid shows three
bands separated by two almost straight, roughly parallel lines (both slope down in x1):

- class 0 / class 1 boundary (bisected on 24 x1 values): x0 = 4.43 - 2.68*x1, max deviation from
  a straight line 0.006 -> effectively linear.
- class 1 / class 2 boundary (49 x1 values): x0 = 6.83 - 1.14*x1, max deviation 0.044 (slight
  curvature, strongest near x1 = 0.1 where the boundary is at x0 = 6.76).
- Classes 0 and 2 never touch inside the box, so the decision is an ordered chain 0 < 1 < 2.

#### What I changed
Replaced the 8 sigmoids and 3 output sums with two linear tests:

    if x0 + 2.7*x1 < 4.45: return 0
    if x0 + 1.125*x1 > 6.8: return 2
    return 1

Snapping: for each boundary I scanned "nice" slopes (2.5 ... 2.8, 8/3; 1.0 ... 1.25) and
thresholds in 0.05 steps, kept the combos that pass the checker, and chose the simple values in the
middle of the passing range with the fewest unsure-zone disagreements (2.7 / 4.45 and
1.125 = 9/8 / 6.8). Slopes 2.5, 1.0 and 1.2 already fail, so the zone is narrow (unsure zone
is 3.9% of the domain) and the slopes cannot be rounded to integers.

Result: PASS (snap), 0 disagreements where the net is confident, 3641 in the unsure zone (mostly
near x1 ~ 0.1, x0 ~ 6.6-6.7, where the real 1/2 boundary bends away from the straight line).
PASS (exact) is not reachable with a straight-line rule, since the net's boundary is a sigmoid
level curve and not exactly straight.

#### Heuristics
- For argmax nets with 2 inputs, first print the argmax on a coarse grid: it shows the region
  topology (which classes border each other, ordered bands vs. wedges) right away.
- Bisect each pairwise boundary (only where the pair is the top two) along one input for many
  values of the other, then fit a line by least squares. The residual tells you if one linear test
  per boundary is enough.
- When the classes are ordered bands, write the program as a decision chain (if A: 0, elif B: 2,
  else 1); the middle class needs no test of its own.
- Write each boundary as `x0 + k*x1 < t` (normalise one coefficient to 1), then snap k and t
  separately: scan a few nice k values and for each the set of passing t, then pick the centre of
  the widest passing range. Check the snap with the checker, not by eye: the allowed slope range
  can be narrow (here +-0.1).
- Sigmoid nets give smooth, slightly curved boundaries. A straight-line rule usually passes snap but
  not exact; the remaining disagreements cluster at the boundary end with the most curvature.
- The many hidden units (8 here) are no hint of complexity: several sigmoids are near-duplicates
  (similar weight directions) that together give one sharp boundary.

</details>

```python
# The network approximates the median of its three inputs, median(a, b, c)
# (correlation 0.998), but its own error against the hard median reaches 0.64
# (near ties and at the box corners), which is beyond the snap tolerance (0.118).
# So this is a compact smooth surrogate of the net: 6 soft comparisons of the
# inputs (instead of 16 + 16 hidden units), a linear pull toward -(sum)/8,
# and one output sigmoid.
def net(a, b, c):
    # soft pairwise / ordering comparisons (biases snapped to 0)
    x0_over_x1 = sigmoid(1.75 * a - 2 * b + 0.25 * c)
    x0_over_x1_b = sigmoid(2 * a - 1.75 * b - 0.25 * c + 0.05)
    x2_over_x0 = sigmoid(-1.5 * a + 0.5 * b + c)
    x2_over_mean01 = sigmoid(1.25 * (c - 0.5 * (a + b)))
    x1_over_x2 = sigmoid(-0.5 * a + 1.75 * b - 1.125 * c)
    x2_step = sigmoid(0.75 * c)
    # the two a-vs-b gates nearly cancel; their difference is a bump at a ~ b tilted by c
    score = (0.625 * x0_over_x1 - 0.6 * x0_over_x1_b
             + 0.625 * x2_over_x0 - 1.2 * x2_over_mean01 - 0.55 * x1_over_x2 + 0.375 * x2_step
             - 0.112 * a - 0.125 * b - 0.125 * c + 0.3531)
    # decreasing squashing back to the output range: about -6.575 * tanh(score / 2)
    return 6.55 - 13.15 * sigmoid(score)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network computes
- Sampling the dump (200k uniform points) and correlating the output with simple candidates: the
  output is close to the **median of the three inputs**, with correlation 0.998 (sum 0.90, max/min 0.58).
- The hard median does not pass, though: `max(min(x0,x1), min(max(x0,x1),x2))` is off by up to 0.64
  (snap tolerance 0.118). The net is a *soft* median. Near ties (two inputs close) it is pulled toward
  the third input by up to ~0.3. At box corners where x0 and x1 are far apart and x2 ties one of them
  (e.g. (-3,3,-3) -> -2.36), the gap reaches 0.64. The error is also not symmetric in the inputs.
- Smooth soft-median formulas didn't pass either. I fitted soft max/min built from sigmoid gates
  (`a*g + b*(1-g)`, g = sigmoid(k(a-b))), both chained and symmetric, minimising the max error.
  The best was 0.35. The net's deviation from the median is idiosyncratic, not a clean temperature.

#### What I did instead (structural compression, then snapping)
1. PCA of the layer-2 pre-activations: 93% of the variance is in one direction. The 16 layer-2 units
   split into two mirror groups: the positive-output units are sign-flipped copies of the negative ones,
   since sigmoid(z) = 1 - sigmoid(-z).
   Replacing them by **one** layer-2 unit (initialised as the group average, then refit) gives a max
   error of 0.061.
2. Layer 1 has 9 "near-linear" units: weights ~0.15 on every input, so they stay in sigmoid's linear
   range. I replaced them with a direct linear term in x inside the score. 6 steep units remain:
   - two x0-vs-x1 gates;
   - x2 vs x0;
   - x2 vs mean(x0,x1);
   - x1 vs x2;
   - a soft step in x2.

   Max error 0.063.
   Ablation:
   - dropping one of the two x0-vs-x1 gates gives 0.24. The two gates have opposite output weights
     and nearly cancel. Their difference is a bump at x0 ~ x1, tilted by x2;
   - dropping x1-vs-x2 or x2-vs-mean gives 0.24 / 0.74;
   - dropping the x2 step gives 0.096.
3. Snapping the whole first layer to a 0.25 grid at once broke it (0.17). Snapping greedily worked.
   The order was:
   - closest-to-grid weight first;
   - fix it, refit all still-free parameters with an L8 loss (a max-error proxy);
   - keep the snap only if the validation max error stays under a threshold;
   - otherwise try a finer grid (0.25 -> 0.125 -> 0.05).

   This brought all first-layer weights to quarter/eighth values and the biases to 0 (one 0.05 left).
   Most mixing weights ended on 0.05-0.125 steps. The x0 linear coefficient (-0.112) and the score
   bias (0.353) would not snap. Forcing the linear term to -sum/8 doubles the error.
4. The output, `6.55 - 13.15*sigmoid(score)`, is about `-6.575*tanh(score/2)`.

Result: `PASS (snap)`, max |program - net| = 0.099 against a tolerance of 0.118. That is 6 sigmoid
comparisons and one output sigmoid, instead of 16+16 hidden units. A less-snapped variant
(more 2-3 digit weights) reached 0.082.
I did not find an exact closed form. The honest reading is "a soft median whose smoothing the net
learned unevenly".

#### Heuristics
- For regression nets, first correlate the output with a library of symmetric candidates
  (sum, max, min, median, products, abs differences). A correlation above 0.99 names the intent even
  when the formula itself won't pass the tolerance.
- Probe the residual (net - candidate) along tie slices: two inputs equal, the third offset by b.
  If it peaks at moderate b and decays, the net is a smooth/soft version of a piecewise function.
  Large residuals at box corners are extrapolation artefacts that no clean formula will match.
- Sigmoid nets: units with small weights on all inputs (|w|*range < ~1.5) sit in the linear part of
  the sigmoid. Fold them into a linear skip term.
- Sigmoid nets: layer-2 units with mirrored weights and opposite-sign output weights are the same
  unit (sigmoid(z) = 1 - sigmoid(-z)). PCA of the pre-activations tells how many distinct units the
  layer really has. Collapse to that many and refit.
- Two near-identical steep units with opposite output weights are not redundant. Their difference is
  a derivative-like bump along the shared boundary, so ablate before pruning.
- Snap greedily and refit the free parameters after each snap, with an L^p loss (p 8-12) to control
  the max error. Snapping a whole layer at once to a coarse grid fails for smooth (sigmoid) nets.
- Keep a margin (~15-30% of the snap tolerance), because the checker adds boundary and corner
  points that a uniform validation set under-samples. Validate on a grid that includes the corners.

</details>

```python
# Smoothed "median of three"-like function, symmetric under swapping a <-> b.
# The 16x16 sigmoid net collapses to: 5 first-layer "ordering" detectors + a linear
# trend, fed into ONE second-layer sigmoid (all 16 layer-2 units were sign-flipped copies).
def net(a, b, c):
    # a / c vs b ordering: "is a (resp. b) above the other two", tilted toward c
    x0_high = sigmoid(1.6 * a - 0.6 * b - c) - 0.5
    x1_high = sigmoid(1.6 * b - 0.6 * a - c) - 0.5
    # gate on b vs a (steep, slope 2), offset +-0.1*(a + b - 2*c): a bump around a == b
    near_tie = sigmoid(-1.9 * a + 2.1 * b - 0.2 * c) - sigmoid(-2.1 * a + 1.9 * b + 0.2 * c)
    # is c above the mean of a, b
    x2_high = sigmoid(0.64 * (2 * c - a - b)) - 0.5
    z = 0.56 * (x0_high + x1_high) + 0.6 * near_tie + x2_high + 0.11 * (a + b) + 0.07 * c
    return 14 * (sigmoid(z) - 0.5)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network computes
Probing showed an odd function, y(-x) ~= -y(x), with y(1,2,3) ~= 2, y(3,-3,1) ~= 1 and y(t,t,0) ~= t.
That is a smoothed **median of three** (least squares: y ~= 0.995*median). It is a rough one, though:
it differs from the true median by up to 0.66 (for example y(t,2,2) runs from 1.63 to 2.18). So the
program has to copy the net's own smooth shape, not the clean median (max(min(a,b),min(max(a,b),c))
is off by 0.66, beyond the 0.115 tolerance).

#### What I changed
1. **Layer 2 collapse.** Flip every layer-2 unit with a negative output weight
   (w*sig(z) = w - w*sig(-z)). After that, all 16 rows have the same sign pattern and similar sizes,
   so layer 2 is one unit copied 16 times. A single sigmoid `A*sigmoid(z)+B` with refitted z holds
   within 0.07 (just averaging the rows gives 0.43, so it needs the refit).
2. **Layer 1 pruning.** 11 of the 16 units have tiny weights (~0.11, 0.11, 0.07) and work in the
   near-linear part of the sigmoid. Together they become one linear term `0.11*(x0+x1) + 0.07*x2`.
   Five units carry the structure.
3. **Centering.** Writing each unit as `sigmoid(.) - 0.5` sends the layer-2 bias to about 0 and the
   output bias to 0, so `y = 14*(sigmoid(z) - 0.5)`, which is odd, as observed.
4. **Snapping the directions** using the pairwise basis (2xi - xj - xk, xj - xk):
   - (1.56,-0.56,-0.97) -> `1.6x0 - 0.6x1 - x2`, and (0.57,-1.59,1.0) is its mirror under x0<->x1
     (it enters with the opposite sign), so the two make one symmetric term `x0_high + x1_high`.
   - (0.64,0.64,-1.26) -> `0.64*(2x2 - x0 - x1)`.
   - (-2.05,1.86,0.2) and (-1.84,2.05,-0.2) -> `2(x1-x0) -/+ 0.1(x0+x1-2x2)`. Their weights are
     opposite (+-0.61), so they are a *difference* of sigmoids: a bump near x0 == x1 scaled by
     (x0+x1-2x2). It looks redundant (two near-copies that cancel), but removing it costs 0.61.
   - Layer-1 biases (all ~0) are dropped.
5. Refit the 6 scalars with directions fixed, then snap them: 0.56, 0.6, 1, 0.11, 0.07, 14.

Result: `PASS (snap)`, max |program - net| = 0.0725 (tolerance 0.1145), from 16+16 units down to
6 sigmoids. Exact is out of reach for a 2-layer sigmoid net without keeping all its weights.

#### Heuristics
- **Probe first:** check the symmetry (odd? which input swaps leave it unchanged?), then a few integer
  points, then compare with candidate formulas (median, max, sum...). Measure the max error against
  the tolerance before trusting a "clean" formula. A trained net can be a rough approximation of
  the clean function, so the program must follow the net, not the idea.
- **Sigmoid sign-flip:** `w*sigmoid(z) = w - w*sigmoid(-z)`. Flip units with negative outgoing weight.
  If the rows then line up, the layer is one unit copied many times. Collapse it to one unit and
  refit its row by least squares; plain averaging is not enough.
- **Near-linear sigmoid units:** units with small weights and small biases (|arg| < ~1 over the
  domain) act linearly. Replace them as a group with one linear term in the inputs, then refit.
- **Centered sigmoids:** use `sigmoid(.) - 0.5` (= tanh(./2)/2). Biases then often vanish and the
  function's oddness shows.
- **Pairs of near-identical units with opposite weights** compute a derivative-like bump
  (sig(a+d) - sig(a-d) ~ 2d*sig'(a)). They are not redundant. Keep them as a difference and test
  before deleting.
- **Snap first-layer directions** in a basis of pairwise differences (2xi - xj - xk, xj - xk, plus the
  sum) for order-statistic tasks (min/max/median), and look for mirror pairs under input swaps to tie
  their coefficients.
- **Order of work:** fix the directions (snapped), refit only the few scalar coefficients by least squares, then
  snap those to 2 significant digits, checking the max error at every step.

</details>

```python
# dumped from a neural network: 2 inputs -> 8 -> 1 outputs
# inputs: a: float [-2, 2], b: float [-2, 2]
# output: binary: class 1 if the returned value > 0, else class 0
def net(a, b):
    h1_0 = sigmoid(0.60991824 * a - 0.05882649 * b + 3.0082078)  # layer 1 neuron 0
    h1_1 = sigmoid(-0.20419079 * a - 0.70128834 * b + 3.831982)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    h1_2 = sigmoid(-0.5207617 * a + 0.1417692 * b - 2.374078)  # layer 1 neuron 2
    h1_3 = sigmoid(-11.391683 * a + 11.432839 * b - 2.4444447)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    h1_4 = sigmoid(0.76856107 * a - 0.6093585 * b + 0.87131363)  # layer 1 neuron 4
    h1_5 = sigmoid(0.07886289 * a - 0.47804934 * b + 3.0389106)  # layer 1 neuron 5
    h1_6 = sigmoid(0.26835608 * a - 0.45068708 * b + 2.0882733)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = sigmoid(-12.280662 * a + 12.273867 * b + 2.4091175)  # layer 1 neuron 7
    return -1.3107773 * h1_0 - 1.4504701 * h1_1 + 0.66854167 * h1_2 - 16.519886 * h1_3 - 1.2155019 * h1_4 - 1.4078876 * h1_5 - 1.3038645 * h1_6 + 14.458104 * h1_7 - 0.85005003
```

```python
def net(a, b):
    # class 1 when the two inputs are within 0.2 of each other
    gap = abs(b - a)
    return gap < 0.2
```

```python
    return -0.6276106120663069 * a + 0.3364050060268133 * b - 7.550908990703522 - 71.79940707360959 * relu(0.9964411735156969 * a - b + 0.08347857406581637) + 50.98529669869073 * relu(0.9998003811397929 * a - b + 0.34839635090182886) + 21.201289837312356 * relu(0.9975220055043533 * a - b - 0.4609964814891104)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Agent notes

#### What the network does

Class 1 exactly when the two inputs are nearly equal: `abs(x1 - x0) < 0.2`.

#### How I got there

- Six hidden units have small weights (|w| < 0.8) and moderate biases. Over the whole input box
  their combined contribution to the output is a nearly flat negative offset (-7.4 to -5.4).
  Its only job is to keep the output below 0 away from the band.
- Two units are steep (|w| about 11-12) and both have weights in the direction x1 - x0, with opposite
  signs on equal-and-opposite coefficients:
  - h1_7 = sigmoid(12.3*(x1 - x0) + 2.41) switches on at x1 - x0 > -0.196 (output weight +14.5);
  - h1_3 = sigmoid(11.4*(x1 - x0) - 2.44) switches on at x1 - x0 > +0.214 (output weight -16.5).
  Together they make a bump (+14.5 minus about 6.4 offset, so positive) on the band
  -0.2 < x1 - x0 < 0.2, and negative everywhere else.
- Root-finding along x0 = const gave the real boundaries at x1 - x0 from -0.203 to -0.200 (lower)
  and +0.195 to +0.209 (upper), drifting slightly with x0 because of the flat units. Both
  snap to one shared half-width of 0.2.
- `abs(x1 - x0) < 0.2` passes the checker as PASS (snap): 0 disagreements where the net is
  confident, and 0.91% of inputs differ only in the unsure zone along the band edges.
- PASS (exact) would mean keeping all 8 sigmoids, including the drift of about 0.01 in the band
  edges. That drift is a training artifact, so I did not keep it.

#### Heuristics

- **Look for steep units first.** In a sigmoid (or ReLU) net with a binary output, the units with
  large input weights (about 10x the others) carry the decision boundary. Small-weight units with
  large biases are usually a near-constant offset. To confirm, evaluate their sum over the domain
  and check that its range is narrow compared with the steep units' output weights.
- **Pairs of steep units with the same direction and opposite output signs form a band (bump).**
  A unit whose weights are (-a, +a) depends only on x1 - x0. Its boundary sits at -bias / a. Two
  such units, one with a positive and one with a negative output weight, give
  lo < x1 - x0 < hi. A symmetric band becomes `abs(x1 - x0) < c`.
- **Snap shared thresholds.** If the two edges of a band are about ±c (here -0.196 / +0.214 from the
  unit biases, and ±0.20 measured), use one symmetric value c, rounded to a simple decimal.
- **Measure the real boundary, not just the unit's switch point.** The constant offset moves where
  the output crosses 0. Root-find the net output along a few lines across the boundary to get the
  actual edges before choosing the snapped constant.
- For a binary output, returning a comparison (1/0) is enough. The checker uses `raw > threshold`,
  so a boolean output of 1 or 0 against threshold 0 works.

</details>

```python
# output: binary: class 1 if the returned value > 0, else class 0

    h1_1 = sigmoid(-10.264247 * a + 10.244508 * b - 2.202513)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    h1_2 = sigmoid(-6.1676908 * a + 6.2344403 * b + 1.8394408)  # layer 1 neuron 2
    h1_3 = sigmoid(-11.394746 * a + 11.374804 * b - 2.3789897)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    h1_4 = sigmoid(-7.186394 * a + 7.168465 * b - 1.816756)  # layer 1 neuron 4
    h1_5 = sigmoid(10.800418 * a - 10.833183 * b - 2.3336902)  # layer 1 neuron 5
    h1_6 = sigmoid(11.521255 * a - 11.549395 * b - 2.4432764)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = sigmoid(0.7019879 * a - 0.62826186 * b + 2.4517581)  # layer 1 neuron 7
    return 0.48466286 * h1_0 - 8.489141 * h1_1 + 4.465596 * h1_2 - 9.370281 * h1_3 - 5.2992163 * h1_4 - 9.283582 * h1_5 - 9.997082 * h1_6 + 3.4543 * h1_7 + 3.1285763
```

AST tool (no LLM, `ast3`):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: kink-merge)
def net(a, b):
    return 0.19119276110668212 * a - 0.30912311668366466 * b - 11.437026754635177 - 57.334747633408064 * relu(a - 0.9993277821801555 * b - 0.009016637196360797) + 47.35281987482058 * relu(0.9972544162629186 * a - b - 0.44292047445041755) - 20.401632080015148 * relu(0.9958862435276313 * a - b + 0.029450522941378576) + 40.19846583144028 * relu(a - 0.9982391896134186 * b + 0.47918562236949425) - 10.799275258468166 * relu(0.9911591677233639 * a - b - 0.21384458226749556)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does

All eight hidden sigmoids except two weak ones (h1_0, h1_7) have weights of the form `k*(x1 - x0)`:
the x0 and x1 coefficients are opposite and almost equal in size (-10.26/+10.24, -6.17/+6.23, ...).
So the net is mostly a function of one variable, `diff = x1 - x0`:

- h1_1, h1_3, h1_4: steep steps that switch on at diff ~ +0.21..0.25, each with a large negative output weight.
- h1_5, h1_6: mirror-image steps that switch on at diff ~ -0.21, also large negative output weight.
- h1_2: a step that switches on at diff ~ -0.30, positive weight. Together with the bias it makes the
  middle band positive.
- h1_0, h1_7: shallow, small, nearly constant over the domain. They only tilt the boundary slightly.

Scanning the net's sign along x1 for fixed x0 gave decision boundaries at diff = -0.198..-0.207 and
+0.1995..+0.2017 across the whole domain, which is a band of half-width 0.2 around the diagonal.

#### Result

    def net(x0, x1):
        diff = x1 - x0
        return 0.2 - abs(diff)

Class 1 means "x0 and x1 are within 0.2 of each other". `check.py`: PASS (snap), 0 disagreements where
the net is confident. The 1.1% unsure-zone disagreements sit right on the band edge, where the net's
boundary drifts by up to about 0.007 (the small tilt from h1_0/h1_7). PASS (exact) would mean
keeping that sigmoid drift, which has no meaning, so I stopped at the snapped rule.

#### Heuristics

- Look for hidden units whose input weights are equal-and-opposite (or proportional across units).
  This means the net depends on one linear combination, such as `x1 - x0`. Rewrite in that variable first.
- For a steep sigmoid `sigmoid(k*(z) + b)`, the switch point is `z = -b/k`. List these and group the units
  that share a direction and threshold into one step. Mirror pairs (`+z` vs `-z`) with similar
  thresholds and same-sign output weights mean an `abs(z)` band.
- Shallow, small-weight units (|k| <~ 2, |output weight| < 1) usually only tilt the boundary a little.
  Try dropping them first.
- For binary outputs, find the boundary directly: scan the sign of the net along lines and read off the
  crossings. Then snap them to a round number (0.2 here) and return a signed margin like
  `0.2 - abs(diff)`. That stays inside the language and is easy to read.

</details>

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does
Binary classifier on two floats in [-2, 2]: class 1 when the inputs are nearly equal,
`|x0 - x1| < 0.2`.

#### How I got there
- All eight hidden sigmoid units have weights on x0 and x1 that are (almost) equal and opposite
  for the large-weight units (h0, h2, h4, h6, h5: ratios within ~1%), so they depend only on
  `d = x0 - x1`. The remaining small-weight units (h1, h3, h7) are nearly flat over the domain and
  only bend the boundary slightly.
- Steep units are soft steps: h0/h2 step up at d ~ -0.245, h4 steps at d ~ +0.19 (weight -18),
  h6 steps at d ~ -0.23 (weight -13). Together they form a band "on" between about -0.2 and +0.2.
- Scanned the decision boundary along d at fixed x0 + x1 from -3.5 to 3.5: the crossings stayed at
  d = -0.203..-0.209 and d = +0.201..+0.203, i.e. a symmetric band of half-width 0.2 with drift
  under 0.01. Snapped to 0.2.
- Final program: `return 0.2 - abs(x0 - x1)` (positive inside the band), a signed margin rather
  than a 1/0 so the score has a meaningful magnitude.
- Check: PASS (snap), 0 disagreements where the net is confident; the 1.25% that disagree all lie
  in the net's unsure zone along the band edges. PASS (exact) is not reachable with a simple
  formula because the sigmoid boundary is slightly curved/asymmetric (by ~0.005).

#### Heuristics
- Look for hidden units whose input weights are equal and opposite (w0 ~ -w1): they depend only on
  the difference x0 - x1 (equal weights: the sum). Rotate coordinates to (difference, sum) and scan.
- For a sigmoid unit `sigmoid(a*z + b)`, the step location is `z = -b/a` and its sharpness is |a|;
  units with |a| * input-range << 4 are nearly linear/constant and can usually be dropped for a
  binary decision.
- A pair of opposite-signed steep steps on the same direction with big output weights forms a band
  (window) detector; read off the two step locations and the output sign to get `lo < z < hi`.
- Locate the actual decision boundary numerically (scan along one direction at several offsets of
  the orthogonal direction) instead of trusting single-unit thresholds; the combined boundary can
  sit away from any one unit's step.
- If the band is symmetric around 0, write it as `abs(z) < c`; return `c - abs(z)` as a signed margin.
- Snap the boundary to a round number (0.2 here) if the measured drift across the domain is
  smaller than the net's unsure zone.

</details>

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Agent notes

#### Result
`agent.py`: `return 0.2 - abs(x1 - x0)`. Class 1 iff |x1 - x0| < 0.2. `check.py`: PASS (snap), with 0
disagreements where the net is confident. The 6419 disagreements are all in the unsure zone (0.99% of inputs).

#### What I changed and why
- **Steep units:** h1_0, h1_3 and h1_5 have weights of about ±10..11 on x0 and x1, with opposite signs and
  equal size, so each one is a soft step in d = x1 - x0. Dividing the bias by the slope puts the steps at
  d ~ -0.21 (h1_0 and h1_3, together +19.5) and d ~ +0.20 (h1_5, -18.2). The two rising steps sit almost
  on top of each other, so I merged them.
- **Slow units:** h1_1, h1_2, h1_4, h1_6 and h1_7 have small weights and large biases (|b| ~ 3-3.8), so
  they stay saturated over the whole box. With the output bias they add up to about -10, varying only by
  ±0.17 (a least-squares linear fit gives -9.99 + 0.048 x0 - 0.038 x1). They are a constant offset.
- **Net shape:** the output is about -10 below the band, +9.5 inside it and -8.7 above it. That is an
  indicator of |d| < 0.2.
- **Measured boundaries:** I scanned the class boundary along d for 13 values of (x0+x1)/2. The lower edge
  is at d = -0.198..-0.203 and the upper edge at +0.198..+0.207. Both snap to ±0.2, a symmetric band.
- **Exact pass not reached:** keeping the three steep sigmoids and fitting the slow units with a quadratic
  still did not reach PASS (exact). It halved the unsure-zone disagreements, but the boundary shifts by
  ~0.005 across the box, and the slow units cause that drift. I kept the simple formula.

#### Heuristics
- Look for hidden units whose input weights are large, equal in size and opposite in sign. They are
  steps in a difference (x1 - x0), placed at -bias/slope. Group units with the same direction and close
  thresholds into one step, and add their output weights.
- Units with small input weights and a large |bias| are saturated on the whole input box. Treat them as
  constants (sigmoid(b) ~ 1 or 0) and fold them into the output bias. Bound how much they vary over the
  box, to confirm they cannot move a decision.
- For binary outputs, add up the plateau values between the steps (below, between, above). A
  "+big then -big" pair with a negative base gives a band indicator: `t - abs(d - c)` when the band is
  symmetric about c.
- Measure the actual decision boundary numerically along the main direction, at several points across
  the orthogonal direction, and snap to the shared nice value (here ±0.2). Do not rely only on
  -bias/slope of each unit, because the summed steps and offsets move the crossing point.
- When the only gap to an exact pass is slow drift from near-saturated units, accept PASS (snap). A
  readable formula is worth more than matching the drift of the boundary.

</details>

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does
Class 1 iff the two inputs are nearly equal: `|x1 - x0| < 0.2`.

#### How I got there
- Six of the eight hidden units (0, 1, 2, 3, 6, 7) have weights of the form `w * (x1 - x0)`
  (the two coefficients agree to within ~0.3%), so they are all functions of one feature
  `d = x1 - x0`. Units 1 and 2 are near-duplicates (same slope ~11.07, same bias ~-2.30).
- Units 1, 2 (rising steps at d ~ +0.21) and unit 3 (a falling step at d ~ -0.20) carry large
  negative output weights (-11, -10, -13): they push the score negative when d is too large
  or too small. Units 0, 6, 7 (soft steps in d with positive weights) and the bias lift the
  middle band above 0.
- Units 4 and 5 have small weights on both inputs and small output weights (0.86, 0.55). They
  only tilt the boundary slightly (the band edges drift by about 0.005 across the domain), so
  I dropped them.
- I scanned the score along d for several fixed values of s = x0 + x1. The sign changes sat at
  d = -0.200..-0.205 and d = +0.195..+0.207 for every s, so the boundary is two parallel lines
  at d = +/-0.2. Snapped both to 0.2 (one shared threshold, symmetric).
- `agent.py` returns `0.2 - abs(x1 - x0)`; it is > 0 exactly in the band.

#### Check
`PASS (snap)`, empirical, 285425 inputs: 0 disagreements where the net is confident, 6117
in the net's unsure zone (all on the band edges). An exact match would need the soft
sigmoid tilts, which are training noise rather than meaning.

#### Heuristics
- Look at the ratio of the input weights in each first-layer unit. When many units share the
  same direction (here w*(x1 - x0)), rewrite the net as a 1-D function of that feature and
  analyse it along that line.
- Units whose input weights and output weight are both small next to the others mostly tilt
  the boundary a little; drop them first and see whether the check still passes.
- Near-duplicate units (same slope and bias) add up to one unit; with large slopes,
  sigmoid(k*(d - t)) acts as a step at d = t = -bias/slope.
- For a binary output, find the sign changes of the score along the main feature at several
  values of the orthogonal coordinate. If they barely move, the boundary is a set of
  axis-parallel cuts in the feature: snap them to round values, and use abs() when the cuts
  are symmetric (`c - abs(d)` for a band, `abs(d) - c` for outside a band).
- A large negative pair of steps on each side plus a positive bias is the motif for
  "class 1 inside a band" (the net's version of `abs(a - b) < c`).

</details>

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What I did
- Five of the eight sigmoid units (h1_0, h1_2, h1_3, h1_4, h1_6) have input weights of the form
  w*(x0 - x1) with |w| about 7.4-10, so they are soft steps in the single variable d = x0 - x1.
  Their thresholds (-bias/w) sit at d = -0.231, +0.197, +0.197, -0.252, -0.229: two groups, one near
  d = -0.2 and one near d = +0.2.
- The other three units (h1_1, h1_5, h1_7) have small weights (< 1) and large positive biases; over
  [-2, 2]^2 they are slowly varying and act mostly as a constant offset, only nudging the boundary
  by a few thousandths.
- Scanning the net along lines x0 + x1 = const showed the sign change at d = -0.199..-0.207 and
  d = +0.198..+0.205 everywhere: the positive class is the band |x0 - x1| < 0.2.
- Final program: `return 0.2 - abs(x0 - x1)`. Checker: PASS (snap), 0 confident disagreements; all
  disagreements are in the net's unsure zone along the band edges (the net's edges drift by up to
  0.007 because of the slow units).
- I did not pursue PASS (exact): it would require keeping the slow sigmoid units to reproduce the
  small boundary drift, which is noise rather than structure.

#### Heuristics
- Group hidden units by the direction of their weight vector. Units whose weights are (w, -w) or
  (-w, w) depend only on the difference x0 - x1; compute each unit's threshold -b/w on that axis.
- Steep sigmoid units (|w| large relative to the input range) are steps; pairs of rising/falling steps
  with opposite output signs at nearly the same threshold usually form one edge of a band.
- Shallow sigmoid units with large biases are near-constant over the input box; treat them as
  part of the output bias first and only keep them if the check fails.
- For binary nets, directly locate the decision boundary by scanning sign changes along lines
  orthogonal to the suspected direction; snap the found thresholds to a simple value (here 0.2)
  and express the region with abs() for symmetric bands.
- A band |a - b| < t can be written as a single return value `t - abs(a - b)`, which keeps the
  output's sign convention (positive = class 1) and gives a margin-like score.

</details>

```python
# dumped from a neural network: 1 inputs -> 8 -> 1 outputs
# inputs: income: float [0, 200]
# output: regression: the returned value
def net(income):
    h1_0 = sigmoid(0.061903752 * income - 5.551672)  # layer 1 neuron 0
    h1_1 = sigmoid(0.0788277 * income - 10.577549)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    h1_2 = sigmoid(-0.090218455 * income + 8.340739)  # layer 1 neuron 2
    h1_3 = sigmoid(-0.07361433 * income + 8.986365)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    h1_4 = sigmoid(0.032237656 * income + 0.18311094)  # layer 1 neuron 4
    h1_5 = sigmoid(0.09007088 * income - 16.314112)  # layer 1 neuron 5
    h1_6 = sigmoid(-0.13689165 * income + 5.2839427)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = sigmoid(0.085465856 * income - 14.363153)  # layer 1 neuron 7
    return 9.8415985 * h1_0 + 9.861274 * h1_1 - 6.757179 * h1_2 - 6.7510643 * h1_3 + 13.752877 * h1_4 + 9.963021 * h1_5 - 3.7615767 * h1_6 + 9.904715 * h1_7 + 9.563072
```

```python
def net(income):
    # Convex piecewise-linear ramp: slope 0.18 below the knee, slope 0.39 above it.
    # The two lines cross at income = 75 (0.18*75 - 0.7 = 0.39*75 - 16.45 = 12.8).
    low_ramp = 0.18 * income - 0.7
    high_ramp = 0.39 * income - 16.45
    return max(low_ramp, high_ramp)
```

```python
    return 0.17480453088088008 * income - 1.0247273606239635 + 0.21216508336580922 * relu(income - 70)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does

One float input x0 in [0, 200], 8 sigmoid hidden units, regression output (range about -0.14 .. 60.6).
Sigmoid centres (-b/w): 89.7, 134.2, 92.5 (falling), 122.1 (falling), -5.7, 181.1, 38.6 (falling),
168.1. Their slopes are small (0.03-0.14), so every unit is a soft ramp several tens of units wide;
the sum is a smooth, monotone increasing, convex-ish curve.

Numerical derivative of the net: about 0.12 near 0, about 0.2 over 30-60, about 0.4 over 90-180, then
it drops to about 0.2 at the far edge (x0 > 185, an edge effect of the last sigmoids saturating).

#### What I changed

- Replaced all 8 sigmoids with a single convex piecewise-linear function:
  `max(0.18*x0 - 0.7, 0.39*x0 - 16.45)`, i.e. slope 0.18 below a knee at x0 = 75 and slope 0.39
  above it (equivalently `0.18*x0 - 0.7 + 0.21*relu(x0 - 75)`).
- Found it by a minimax search over one-knot ramps with snapped slopes (multiples of 0.01/0.025) and
  knots on multiples of 5, intercept chosen as the mid-range of the residual. Max error 0.914 vs a
  snap tolerance of 1.216 (2% of the output range). The worst point is x0 = 200 where the net bends
  down.
- Tried round slopes 0.2 / 0.4: `max(0.2*x0 - 1, 0.4*x0 - 18)` misses by 1.36 at x0 = 200 (fails).
  Adding a third line for the top-end flattening (`min(max(0.18x-0.75, 0.4x-18), 0.2x+20)`) only
  brings the error to 0.76, so it was not worth the extra node. Two knots (75 and 195) reduce it to
  0.77; three knots (35, 80, 190) to 0.47 - none are needed for a pass.
- Result: `PASS (snap)`, max |program - net| = 0.914. An exact pass is not reachable without keeping
  the sigmoids.

#### Heuristics

- For 1-input sigmoid nets, compute each unit's centre `-b/w` and width `1/|w|`; if widths are large
  relative to the gaps between centres, the net is a smooth curve and no single unit is meaningful -
  fit the whole curve instead of simplifying unit by unit.
- Plot / tabulate the numerical derivative on a grid. Plateaus of the derivative are linear pieces;
  transitions mark knots. Here: plateaus near 0.2 and 0.4 gave a 2-piece ramp.
- Fit piecewise-linear forms with knots on a nice grid (multiples of 5), solve slopes by least
  squares, then snap slopes and search intercept by minimax (intercept = mid-range of residual).
  Compare the resulting max error with the snap tolerance (2% of the output range).
- Write a convex piecewise-linear function as `max(line1, line2, ...)` (concave as `min`); it is the
  shortest form and makes the knot implicit.
- The error is usually largest at the domain edges, where saturating sigmoids bend the curve; check
  the endpoints first and add an edge piece only if the tolerance requires it.
- Prefer the fewest pieces that pass with a comfortable margin over rounder coefficients that fail
  (0.39 passed, the rounder 0.4 did not because of the edge).

</details>

```python
def net(income):
    # Progressive "bracket" schedule: marginal rate 0.1 below 20, 0.2 from 20 to 80, 0.4 above 80.
    bracket_1 = 0.1 * income
    bracket_2 = 0.1 * relu(income - 20)
    bracket_3 = 0.2 * relu(income - 80)
    return bracket_1 + bracket_2 + bracket_3 - 0.2
```

```python
    return 0.17708224366721065 * income - 1.1464757754701205 + 0.21109639548053244 * relu(income - 70)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network computes
1 float input x0 in [0, 200], regression output from 0 to about 61, 8 sigmoid hidden units.
The slope of the net gives the shape: about 0.10 near 0, about 0.2 over 30-60, about 0.4 over
90-180, and a bend down to about 0.16 at the very top end (x0 > 190).

So it is a continuous piecewise-linear "progressive rate" function (like tax brackets):
marginal rate 0.1 up to 20, 0.2 from 20 to 80, 0.4 above 80.

#### What I changed
- Replaced the 8 sigmoids with three ReLU terms: `0.1*x0 + 0.1*relu(x0-20) + 0.2*relu(x0-80) - 0.2`.
- Rates snapped to 0.1 / 0.2 / 0.4 (each one doubles); knots snapped to 20 and 80. Many knot pairs
  fit about equally well (the max error is set by the top end), so I chose round ones.
- The constant -0.2 is a centring offset. Without it the error at x0=200 is too large (1.34 > 1.21).
- The bend after 190 looks like the sigmoids saturating at the domain edge, so I left it out.
  Adding `-0.2*relu(x0-190)` cuts the max error to about 0.99, at the cost of one more term.
- Check: PASS (snap), max |program - net| = 1.188 against a tolerance of 1.215 (2% of range).
  The worst point is near x0 = 181, where the sigmoid net overshoots and then bends down.

#### Heuristics
- For a 1-input regression net, sample the net's numeric derivative on a grid first. Plateaus in the
  slope are linear segments; the jumps between them are ReLU knots. With sigmoid hidden units, each
  unit is a soft step in slope. Center = -b/w, and the slope change is about amp*w/4 per unit.
- Fit free-knot continuous piecewise-linear models (minimise the max error) with 2, 3, 4 knots.
  Then snap the slopes to a clean pattern (0.1/0.2/0.4) and grid-search round knots plus a constant
  offset against the max error.
- Sigmoid nets smooth corners and droop at the domain edges. The worst error is usually at a
  smoothed corner or at the boundary, so centre the program with a small constant offset (the
  midpoint of min and max residual) rather than forcing f(0)=0.
- If several knot pairs give the same max error, the knots are not identified by the net. Pick the
  roundest values.
- A final slope change right at the domain edge (here at 190 of 200) is likely edge saturation, not
  part of the function. Keep it only if the check fails without it.

</details>

```python
    h1_3 = sigmoid(0.04846426 * income - 1.4388247)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    h1_4 = sigmoid(0.08358068 * income - 15.05718)  # layer 1 neuron 4
    h1_5 = sigmoid(0.09484065 * income - 9.157592)  # layer 1 neuron 5
    h1_6 = sigmoid(0.03689437 * income - 2.3909726)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = sigmoid(-0.079839215 * income + 11.717677)  # layer 1 neuron 7
    return 9.08509 * h1_0 - 4.7618613 * h1_1 + 8.860044 * h1_2 + 9.42756 * h1_3 + 8.667826 * h1_4 + 8.917195 * h1_5 + 9.680103 * h1_6 - 7.0204425 * h1_7 + 8.89087
```

```python
# Two-rate piecewise-linear ramp (like a two-bracket rate schedule):
#   rate 0.2 per unit of x up to the kink at x = 87.5, rate 0.4 above it.
# The network is a smooth sigmoid approximation of this; the largest deviations
# are at the kink and at the two ends of the domain (sigmoid saturation).
def net(income):
    base = 0.2 * income - 1.25
    extra = 0.2 * relu(income - 87.5)
    return base + extra
```

AST tool (no LLM, `ast3`):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: kink-merge)
def net(income):
    return max(0.17800372947995993 * income - 1, 0.3880037294799599 * income - 16.75)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network is
1 input (x0 in [0, 200]) -> 8 sigmoid units -> linear output (regression).

Each sigmoid unit `sigmoid(w*x + b)` is a soft step centred at x = -b/w with width ~1/|w|.
Centres: 29.7, 64.8, 96.6, 114.7 (falling), 129.4, 146.8 (falling), 179.1, 180.1.
Falling units rewrite as `c*sigmoid(-z) = c - c*sigmoid(z)`, so all eight are rising steps of
height ~4.8-9.7 spread across the domain: a staircase smoothed into a ramp.

Sampling the net's slope shows two flat plateaus of the derivative:
- slope ~0.19-0.20 on roughly [30, 65],
- slope ~0.39-0.41 on roughly [95, 175],
with a smooth transition around x = 85-90 and slope roll-off at both ends (sigmoid
saturation: slope 0.10 at x=0, 0.21 at x=200).

#### What I wrote
`f(x) = 0.2*x - 1.25 + 0.2*relu(x - 87.5)`, i.e. `max(0.2x - 1.25, 0.4x - 18.75)`:
a two-rate linear schedule, rate 0.2 below the kink at 87.5 and 0.4 above it.

Procedure: fix the slopes at snapped values (0.2, then +0.2), scan the kink position on a
fine grid, take the midrange offset (minimax), then snap kink and offset to half/quarter
steps (87.5, -1.25). Max |program - net| = 1.03 vs snap tolerance 1.215 -> PASS (snap).

Not exact: an exact match would need the 8 sigmoids themselves. The residual is
concentrated at the ends of the domain and at the kink, where the sigmoid net is rounded.
A 4-hinge version (slopes 0.1 / +0.1 at 20 / +0.2 at 80 / -0.2 at 190) cuts the error to
~0.47 but models only edge artefacts of the sigmoid fit, so I kept the 2-piece form.

#### Heuristics
- For 1-input sigmoid nets, compute each unit's centre `-b/w` and width `1/|w|`; flip falling
  units (`c*sigmoid(-z) = c - c*sigmoid(z)`) so all are rising steps and fold the constants.
- Many sigmoid steps of similar height spread over the domain mean "ramp", not "staircase":
  look at the numerical derivative of the net rather than at individual units.
- Plateaus in the derivative give the snapped slopes; the places where the derivative moves
  between plateaus give the kinks -> write as `a*x + c + d*relu(x - k)` (or max/min of lines).
- Fit with slopes fixed at snapped values, then choose the offset as the midrange of the
  residual (minimax), not the least-squares mean: the checker bounds the max error.
- Sigmoid nets roll off at domain edges (saturating units); expect the worst errors at the
  ends and at kinks, and do not add hinges just to model that rounding unless needed to pass.
- Check margin vs snap tolerance (2% of output range) before snapping the offset; scan a few
  snapped offsets/kinks (quarter/half steps) and keep the one with the lowest max error.

</details>

```python
def net(income):
    # piecewise-linear "bracket" schedule: rate 0.2 above 10, an extra 0.2 above 80,
    # and the extra rate removed again above 190 (where the net flattens at the domain edge)
    base = relu(income - 10)
    upper = relu(income - 80) - relu(income - 190)
    return 0.2 * base + 0.2 * upper
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the net does
1 input x0 in [0, 200], 8 sigmoid units, regression output (range about 0 .. 60.7).
Sampling the net densely and looking at its numerical slope showed a smooth piecewise-linear shape:

- slope ~0 at x0=0 rising to 0.2 by x0~30 (a smoothed kink near x0=10; y(0)~0);
- slope 0.2 on ~30..65 (y ~ 0.2*x0 - 2);
- slope 0.4 on ~95..175 (y ~ 0.4*x0 - 18); the two lines meet at x0 = 80;
- slope falls back to ~0.2 near the top of the domain (190..200).

So the net is a "bracket schedule": 0.2 per unit above 10, plus another 0.2 per unit above 80,
with the extra rate switched off above ~190 (probably a sigmoid-saturation edge effect, but it
is needed to stay within tolerance at x0=200).

#### What I changed
- Replaced the 8 sigmoid units with three ReLU hinges:
  `0.2*relu(x0-10) + 0.2*(relu(x0-80) - relu(x0-190))`.
- Unit-level observations (not kept as code): h1 (center at x0 = -299) is a near-constant 1
  over the domain; the other units are soft steps centred at 33, 93, 95, 120, 142, 174, 178;
  pairs of opposite-sign soft steps (e.g. 93/95, 174/178) build the slope changes, i.e. the
  sigmoids together approximate integrated steps (ramps).
- Knots snapped to multiples of 10, slopes to 0.2, intercept 0 (the knot at 10 absorbs it).
- Checked: `PASS (snap)`, max |program - net| = 0.905 (tolerance 1.214), worst at the
  smoothed kink x0=10. Dropping the 190 knot fails (error 1.34 at x0=200). Exact pass would
  require keeping the sigmoids (net is smooth, no simple exact form).

#### Heuristics
- For a 1-input regression net, plot/sample the output and its numerical derivative first:
  plateaus in the slope reveal a piecewise-linear target; slope transitions mark knots.
- Sigmoid nets fit kinks by smooth ramps; write the line for each plateau (y = a*x + b),
  intersect consecutive lines to get the knot, then snap knots and slopes to round values.
- A unit whose centre (-b/w) lies far outside the input domain is a constant; fold it into the bias.
- Expect edge effects near domain bounds (saturation makes the slope sag); an extra hinge near
  the edge may be needed to stay within tolerance even if the "true" rule has no such knot.
- Do a small grid search over snapped candidates (knots on a 5/10 grid, slopes in multiples
  of 0.1/0.2) scoring max error against the net, and keep the simplest under the snap tolerance
  with margin.
- The worst error of a hinge approximation to a sigmoid net sits at the knot where the net's
  transition is widest (lowest |w| of the units involved); check those first.

</details>

```python
    h1_7 = sigmoid(0.07901946 * income - 8.017792)  # layer 1 neuron 7
    return 11.410747 * h1_0 + 0.083200194 * h1_1 - 7.587355 * h1_2 + 16.398783 * h1_3 - 13.216715 * h1_4 - 7.8798323 * h1_5 + 11.339807 * h1_6 + 11.278129 * h1_7 + 11.522534
```

```python
def net(income):
    # Progressive (tax-bracket-like) piecewise-linear rate on income, capped at 60:
    #   10% on [0, 20], 20% on [20, 80], 40% above 80.
    low = 0.1 * income
    mid = 0.2 * income - 2
    high = 0.4 * income - 18
    return min(max(low, mid, high), 60)
```

AST tool (no LLM, `ast3`):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: knot-fit)
def net(income):
    return 0.17188822651252814 * income - 1.0523654807506246 + 0.216491556695547 * relu(income - 70)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network computes

The dump is 1 input -> 8 sigmoid units -> 1 linear output on x0 in [0, 200]. Each sigmoid is a soft
step at centre c = -b/w (36, 90, 101, 128, 150, 179, plus one at -18 that is almost constant on
the domain and one at 4.8 with a negligible weight of 0.08). The sum of these steps is a smooth
increasing curve, not a staircase, so I tabulated the output and its derivative instead of
reading the units one by one.

The derivative shows three flat plateaus: about 0.1 on [0, 15], about 0.2 on [35, 70], and about 0.4
on [100, 180], with smooth transitions in between. Then it falls towards 0.14 at x0 = 200. So the
function is a convex piecewise-linear curve with rates 0.1 / 0.2 / 0.4, like progressive tax brackets:

- 0.1*x0 on [0, 20]
- 0.2*x0 - 2 on [20, 80] (continuous at 20: both give 2)
- 0.4*x0 - 18 above 80 (continuous at 80: both give 14)

Lines fitted on the plateaus gave intercepts of about 0, -2 and -17.9, so I snapped them to 0, -2 and -18.
The knots then fall at round values, 20 and 80. Written as `max(...)` of the three lines, it needs
no branches, because the curve is convex.

Without a cap the error at x0 = 200 is 1.57, which is above the 1.21 snap tolerance (2% of the
output range of about 60.4). The net flattens near the top of the domain, so I added `min(..., 60)`.
The top line hits 60 at x0 = 195. This brings the max error down to 0.70, at x0 = 182, where the soft
sigmoid corner rounds the kink.

#### Result

`agent.py`: `min(max(0.1*x0, 0.2*x0 - 2, 0.4*x0 - 18), 60)`. Check: PASS (snap), max |diff| 0.704,
tolerance 1.209. PASS (exact) is not possible: a sum of sigmoids never has true kinks or plateaus.

#### Heuristics

- For 1-input sigmoid regression nets, tabulate the output and its numerical derivative across the
  domain. Plateaus in the derivative are the linear pieces. Their slopes are often simple
  numbers (0.1, 0.2, 0.4).
- A sigmoid's centre is -b/w and its width is about 4/|w|. A decreasing sigmoid with a negative
  output weight is an increasing step plus a constant. Units centred well outside the domain are
  constants, or near-linear pieces at the domain edge. Units with tiny output weights can be dropped.
- When the derivative rises step by step, the curve is convex: write it as `max` of lines and no
  branches are needed. When it falls step by step, use `min`.
- Fit each line on its plateau and snap the intercept. Then check that adjacent lines meet at round
  knots (here 20 and 80). Continuity at round knots is good evidence that the snap is right.
- A slope that fades at the edge of the domain often hides a cap or saturation. Try `min(..., round
  value)` before trying extra segments.
- Measure the error against the snap tolerance (2% of the output range) before and after each
  simplification. The worst error usually sits at a soft corner, which a sigmoid net can only
  approximate.

</details>

```python
def net(income):
    h1_0 = sigmoid(0.08169497 * income - 11.303006)  # layer 1 neuron 0
    h1_1 = sigmoid(-0.08810667 * income + 15.51726)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    h1_2 = sigmoid(0.046411935 * income + 2.4653497)  # layer 1 neuron 2
    h1_3 = sigmoid(0.070200615 * income - 6.788423)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    h1_4 = sigmoid(0.08831822 * income - 15.62757)  # layer 1 neuron 4
    h1_5 = sigmoid(-0.07465082 * income + 9.704204)  # layer 1 neuron 5
    h1_6 = sigmoid(-0.10086226 * income + 9.484457)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = sigmoid(0.07273815 * income - 2.7647653)  # layer 1 neuron 7
    return 10.62973 * h1_0 - 7.9049172 * h1_1 + 9.958709 * h1_2 + 10.595965 * h1_3 + 10.447429 * h1_4 - 6.25257 * h1_5 - 6.210617 * h1_6 + 10.472487 * h1_7 + 10.608412
```

```python
def net(income):
    # Piecewise-linear ramp with knots at 20, 80 and 195:
    #   slope 0.1 on [0, 20], 0.2 on [20, 80], 0.4 on [80, 195], 0.2 on [195, 200].
    # Convex part (rising slopes) is a max of lines; the final slowdown is a min cap.
    rising = max(0.1 * income, 0.2 * income - 2, 0.4 * income - 18)
    return min(rising, 0.2 * income + 21)
```

AST tool (no LLM, `ast3`):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: knot-fit)
def net(income):
    return 0.1874276644026351 * income - 0.8580347171032285 + 0.20683579522221054 * relu(income - 80)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does

1 input x0 in [0, 200], regression, 8 sigmoid hidden units. Output rises from about 0.05 to 60.5.

Reading the units as smooth steps (center = -bias/weight):

- h7: step up of 10.5 at x ~ 38
- h3 + h6: together a step up of ~16.8 at x ~ 94-97 (h6 is a falling sigmoid with a negative
  weight, which is the same as a rising step plus a constant)
- h0 + h5: together ~16.9 at x ~ 130-138
- h1 + h4: together ~18.4 at x ~ 176-177
- h2: center at x ~ -53, so over the domain it is nearly constant (a slow drift of under 1)

The steps are wide (1/slope ~ 11-14) and spaced ~40 apart, so they add up to a smooth ramp rather
than a staircase. The numerical derivative shows the shape: ~0.2 on about [30, 65], rising to ~0.4
around x ~ 80-90, ~0.4 (with ripple 0.38-0.46 from the discrete steps) up to ~180, then falling
off toward 0.17 at x = 200. Near x = 0 the slope is only ~0.08 (the edge of the first step).

#### What I wrote (agent.py)

A continuous piecewise-linear function with snapped slopes and knots:

    rising = max(0.1*x0, 0.2*x0 - 2, 0.4*x0 - 18)   # slopes 0.1 / 0.2 / 0.4, knots at 20 and 80
    return min(rising, 0.2*x0 + 21)                  # slope back to 0.2 after x = 195

Check: PASS (snap), max |program - net| = 0.669 against a tolerance of 1.21.

How I got there:

1. Tabulated the net and its derivative to see the slope profile.
2. Minimax (linear-programming) fits of `c + a*x + sum b_k relu(x - k)` for a few knot sets. A
   single knot at 80 already gets 0.91; knots at (20, 80, 185) get ~0.49.
3. Grid search with slopes restricted to nice values (0.1, 0.2, 0.4, ...) and knots on multiples
   of 5. Best: slopes 0.1 / 0.2 / 0.4 / 0.2 with knots 20 / 80 / 195, error 0.56 with a free
   constant of -0.11. Dropping the constant to 0 costs only 0.11, so it was dropped.
4. Rewrote the relu sum as max of lines (convex part) and a min (the final concave bend), since
   each line then has integer intercepts that check against each other (0.2*80-2 = 0.4*80-18 = 14,
   0.4*195-18 = 0.2*195+21 = 60).

Probable underlying rule: a two-rate ramp, 0.2*x below x = 80 and 0.4*x - 16 above it (like a
bracketed rate). The 0.1 piece near 0 and the cap after 195 look like sigmoid edge effects: the net
cannot make a sharp slope at the ends of the domain. Without them the error is 1.7-2.5, beyond the
tolerance, so they stay in to match the net.

Simpler options that also pass but with less margin: `0.19*x + 0.2*relu(x-80) - 0.78` (error 1.07).

#### Heuristics

- For 1-input sigmoid nets, compute each unit's center (-b/w) and width (1/|w|). A falling sigmoid
  with a negative output weight is a rising step plus a constant: `-a*sigmoid(-z) = a*sigmoid(z) - a`.
  Pair units with nearby centers into one step.
- A unit whose center lies far outside the input domain is nearly constant or nearly linear there;
  fold it into the constant / slope.
- Many wide steps spaced closer than about 3-4 widths add up to a ramp, not a staircase. Look at the
  numerical derivative: plateaus in the derivative are linear pieces, ripple between them is an
  artifact of discrete units.
- Fit `c + a*x + sum b_k relu(x - k)` by minimax (linprog over the max abs error), since the snap
  check is a max-error check. Then snap slopes to simple values and knots to multiples of 5 or 10,
  grid-searching over the snapped values and taking the midrange constant.
- Try dropping the constant: if it is small compared with the slack, 0 is nicer.
- Write convex piecewise-linear parts as `max(line, line, ...)` and concave bends as `min(...)`;
  with snapped slopes the intercepts usually become integers and the knots can be read off.
- Sigmoid nets soften slopes at the domain edges; expect extra short pieces at the ends that the
  "true" rule probably does not have, but keep them if the check needs them.

</details>

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does
One float input x0 in [-2, 2], binary output, 4 sigmoid hidden units.

- All four hidden units are steep sigmoids (slope ~10.5-12.3) with centres at
  x0 = 5.336/10.604 = 0.503, 6.191/12.324 = 0.502, -5.248/10.608 = -0.495 (decreasing unit,
  "x0 < -0.495") and -5.209/10.547 = -0.494.
- Units 0 and 1 are duplicates (both "x0 > ~0.5", negative output weight ~-9): together they push the
  score far below zero for x0 > 0.5.
- Unit 2 ("x0 < -0.5", weight -9) pushes the score down for x0 < -0.5.
- Unit 3 ("x0 > -0.5", weight +8.3) plus bias 0.63 makes the score positive in between.
- Scanning the net on a fine grid gives exactly two decision boundaries: x0 = -0.501 and x0 = 0.5025;
  class 1 inside, class 0 outside.

#### Program
`return abs(x0) < 0.5` -- the two boundaries snapped to one shared threshold, +-0.5 (a half step).
Check: PASS (snap), 0 disagreements where the net is confident; all differences are in the net's
unsure zone around the boundaries (the net's own boundaries sit at -0.501 / +0.5025).

#### Heuristics
- For sigmoid units, the centre is -b/w and the steepness is |w|; with |w| >~ 10 over a range of 4,
  treat each unit as a step function at its centre, with direction given by the sign of w.
- Units with nearly equal centres and same direction are duplicates; sum their output weights.
- For 1-input binary nets, the fastest route is to scan the net on a dense grid, list the sign
  changes of the score, and snap them; symmetric boundaries (-a, +a) become `abs(x) < a`.
- A decreasing step "x < -a" is 1 - "x > -a"; recognising this pairs units 2 and 3 into a single
  boundary, which explains why the output weights look like +-9 with a small bias.
- Snap boundaries to half-steps (0.5) when the net's boundaries are within ~0.5% of them; the
  checker's unsure zone absorbs the residual offset.

</details>

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: boundary-scan)
def net(x):
    return abs(x) < 0.5
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does

One input `x0` in [-2, 2], 4 sigmoid hidden units, binary output (class 1 if score > 0).

The four hidden units are steep sigmoid steps (slopes about ±10..13). Their centres (`-bias/weight`) are:

| unit | centre  | direction | output weight |
|------|---------|-----------|---------------|
| h0   | +0.4954 | rising    | -11.08        |
| h1   | +0.4918 | falling   | +3.03         |
| h2   | -0.5032 | falling   | -9.55         |
| h3   | -0.5031 | rising    | +4.41         |

So there are two step pairs: one pair at about +0.5 and one at about -0.5. At +0.5 both units push the
score down as x0 rises past it. At -0.5 both push it down as x0 falls past it. Between them the score is
positive (up to about +7). Outside, it is negative (down to about -7). The net is a band (window)
detector: class 1 when x0 is in roughly (-0.5, 0.5).

I found the exact decision boundaries by bisecting the dumped function: x0 = -0.5069905 and
x0 = 0.4969275. The band is shifted about 0.005 to the left of centre and is slightly asymmetric.

#### Versions

1. Snapped: `return abs(x0) < 0.5`. Result: PASS (snap). It disagrees only in the unsure zone, within
   about 0.007 of the boundaries.
2. Final (`agent.py`): `return -0.5069905 < x0 < 0.4969275`. Result: PASS (exact). The remaining
   listed disagreements all sit at x0 ≈ -0.506991, the float32 tie at the boundary. Moving the
   threshold by 1e-6 in either direction made the count worse, so the current value is the float32
   crossing.

The meaningful formula is `abs(x0) < 0.5` ("x0 within half a unit of 0"). The exact version keeps the
net's measured edges.

All of the sigmoid arithmetic was removed. A single chained comparison replaces it.

#### Heuristics

- For sigmoid or ReLU units on one input, compute each unit's centre `-b/w` first. Units whose centres
  coincide (within about 0.01) form one step, so add their output weights (sign-adjusted by direction)
  together.
- A steep sigmoid (|w| ≥ ~10 over an input range of a few units) acts as a hard step. For binary
  outputs the whole net then reduces to a set of thresholds on the input.
- Two opposite step groups whose centres are symmetric (±c) with output weights of the same sign
  mean a band or window: `abs(x) < c` or `abs(x) > c`. Check the sign of the score at the domain
  ends and in the middle.
- Find the real decision boundary by bisecting the net's score, not from the unit centres. The
  output bias and the soft overlap of the sigmoids move the crossing away from the centres (here by
  up to 0.01).
- Write the snapped version first (`abs(x0) < 0.5`). If you want PASS (exact), replace the snapped
  thresholds with the bisected crossings, and keep the snapped formula in the notes as the meaning.
- When nudging a threshold by 1e-6 makes the checker's disagreement count worse in both directions,
  you are at the float32 crossing. Stop there.

</details>

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does

One float input x0 in [-2, 2], binary output (class 1 when the score > 0). The 4 sigmoid units are
two pairs of steep steps:

- h1_0 = sigmoid(-12.54 x0 + 6.49): a falling step at x0 = 6.49/12.54 = 0.518
- h1_2 = sigmoid(12.07 x0 - 6.27): a rising step at x0 = 0.519, weight -8.0
- h1_3 = sigmoid(-12.99 x0 - 6.42): a falling step at x0 = -0.494, weight -13.2
- h1_1 = sigmoid(3.84 x0 - 2.54): a shallow ramp with a small weight (-0.71); it only shifts the
  score a little and does not create a crossing.

Scanning the score over [-2, 2] gives exactly two sign changes, found by bisection at
x0 = -0.4990145 and x0 = 0.5034656. The score is negative outside (about -6.4 at the left end,
-8.4 at the right end) and positive inside (+6.75 at x0 = 0). So the net is a band (window)
detector: class 1 iff -0.499 < x0 < 0.503.

#### What I changed

- Replaced the 4 sigmoids with a single interval test.
- Snapped both edges to +-1/2 (they are within 0.001 and 0.0035 of it), which makes the band
  symmetric: `abs(x0) < 0.5`.
- Return +1 / -1 so the decision `raw > 0` gives the class.

Check: `PASS (snap)`, 0 disagreements where the net is confident; all disagreements are in the
thin slivers between 0.5 and 0.50347 and between -0.49901 and -0.5, inside the net's unsure zone.

An unsnapped variant `return 1 if -0.49901453 < x0 < 0.50346557 else -1` gives `PASS (exact)`;
I kept the snapped `abs(x0) < 0.5` as the final version because it is the readable rule the net
approximates.

#### Heuristics

- For a 1-input binary net, do not decode the units first: scan the score densely over the input
  range, list the sign changes, and bisect each one to full precision. The decision is then an
  interval rule whose number of intervals equals the number of crossings + 1.
- Read each sigmoid unit as a step at x = -b/w with sharpness |w|. Steep units with large output
  weights carry the decision; a shallow unit with a small output weight is a bias correction and
  can be dropped.
- Two steps of opposite direction with opposite-sign output weights form a bump (window). Rising
  step with negative weight and falling step with negative weight around a positive bias = "inside
  the band" detector.
- When the two edges of a band are near +-c, snap them to a symmetric pair and write `abs(x) < c`.
  Prefer simple fractions (1/2) for edges on a [-2, 2] float range.
- If a snapped threshold fails, keep the bisected value with ~8 significant digits: that usually
  gives PASS (exact) for binary outputs, since disagreements then only sit within float noise.
- Return +-1 instead of the raw score for a binary rule; only the sign matters.

</details>

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Agent notes

#### What the network does

Output is binary (class 1 iff score > 0), one input x0 in [-2, 2]. Each hidden sigmoid has a
steep slope (|w| 10-14), so each is effectively a step at x = -b/w:

| unit | step at | direction | output weight |
|------|---------|-----------|---------------|
| h1_0 | -0.5034 | falls (1 for x < t) | -7.93 |
| h1_1 | -0.5041 | falls | -7.85 |
| h1_2 | +0.4925 | rises (1 for x > t) | -5.68 |
| h1_3 | +0.4964 | falls | +9.23 |

h1_0 and h1_1 are near-duplicate steps (same direction, thresholds 0.0007 apart) and merge into
one step of weight -15.78 at about -0.5. h1_2 and h1_3 sit at about +0.5 and both push the score down
for x > 0.5. The score is piecewise constant:

- x < -0.5: -15.78 + 9.23 - 1.76 = -8.3 (class 0)
- -0.5 < x < 0.5: 9.23 - 1.76 = +7.47 (class 1)
- x > 0.5: -5.68 - 1.76 = -7.44 (class 0)

The net's exact zero crossings (bisected) are x = -0.49496 and x = +0.49552. Both are within 0.005
of +-1/2, so I snapped them to one shared threshold, giving a symmetric band.

#### Result

`agent.py`: `return 0.5 - abs(x0)`, so class 1 iff |x0| < 1/2.
`check.py` says PASS (snap): 0 disagreements where the net is confident. All disagreements are in
x0 in (-0.5, -0.495) and (0.4955, 0.5), inside the net's unsure zone.

I also checked an unsnapped variant with the raw thresholds, `-0.4949588 < x0 < 0.4955232`
(scratch/exact.py). It gets PASS (exact), but it only copies the net's training noise, so I kept the
snapped formula.

#### Heuristics

- For a 1-input binary net, first scan the score on a dense grid and bisect its sign changes. The
  number and positions of the crossings tell you the whole decision rule before you read the units.
- A sigmoid with |w| times the input range >> 1 is a step at -b/w. Write each unit as (threshold,
  direction, output weight). Then the score is a sum of steps, and the plateau values show which
  units matter.
- Steps in the same direction whose thresholds are within about 1% of the range are duplicates: add
  their output weights and treat them as one step.
- A band of class 1 between two crossings at -t and +t (to within a few thousandths) is |x| < t.
  Write it with abs and one shared threshold. Returning a margin-like score (t - abs(x)) keeps the
  decision `raw > 0` readable.
- Snap thresholds to the nearest half-integer or simple fraction when the offset is well below the
  net's transition width (here about 0.005 against a sigmoid width of about 0.1). The checker's unsure
  zone takes up such offsets.
- An unsnapped program with the bisected crossings gives an exact pass for reference. It shows the
  snap only moves the boundary, not the topology.

</details>

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the net does
1 input x0 in [-2, 2], binary output, 4 sigmoid units.
- h1_0 (rising step at x0 = 6.548/13.332 = 0.491, weight -13.08) and h1_1 (rising step at
  x0 = -6.372/12.436 = -0.512, weight +7.21) form a bump: the score goes up near x0 = -0.5
  and comes down near x0 = +0.5.
- h1_2 and h1_3 are two near-duplicate falling steps (at x0 = -0.529 and -0.526, weights
  -3.93 and -4.46). Together they act as one step of weight -8.38 that pushes the score further
  down for x0 < -0.5. That only deepens the "class 0" region on the left and does not move the
  decision boundary much.
- Scanning the net's score on a fine grid gives exactly two sign changes, at x0 = -0.4968 and
  x0 = +0.4983. The score is positive (up to 6.77) between them and negative (down to -8.74)
  outside.

#### What I changed
- Replaced the whole network with the band rule `class 1 iff |x0| < 1/2`, written as the
  margin `0.5 - abs(x0)` (positive inside the band).
- Snapped the two boundaries (-0.4968, +0.4983) to the symmetric half-step ±0.5.
- Result: PASS (snap). The only disagreements (0.97% of inputs) are in the 0.003-wide slivers
  between the net's boundaries and ±0.5, where the net's score is within the unsure zone.
  Keeping the raw thresholds would give an exact match, but it would only fit training noise.

#### Heuristics
- For 1-input binary nets, skip reading the units one at a time: scan the score densely, find
  the sign changes, and snap each one. The units only matter for explaining the shape.
- Two zero crossings at roughly ±c usually mean an `abs(x) < c` (or `abs(x) > c`) band. Snap
  to a symmetric threshold and write it as the margin `c - abs(x)`, which is shorter than an
  if/else.
- Sigmoid units with almost the same step location (-b/w) and the same weight sign are one
  step split in two. Add their weights.
- A sigmoid step with a large weight that sits far from every zero crossing (only making
  confident regions more confident) does not affect the decision and can be dropped.
- The step location of sigmoid(w*x + b) is -b/w. Compare it with the score's actual zero
  crossings: the boundary falls where the weighted steps balance, not at any single unit's
  step.
- Before snapping a threshold, check that the net's score at the snapped value is inside the
  unsure zone (here about -0.13 and -0.08 against a 0.44 zone).

</details>

### Notes

#### What the network does
Four sigmoid units, each a steep step (slopes ~10-11.4) at a threshold of -bias/weight:
- h1_0: on for x0 < -0.491 (output weight -8.54)
- h1_3: on for x0 > -0.493 (output weight +9.06)
- h1_1: on for x0 > 0.504 (output weight -8.60)
- h1_2: on for x0 > 0.5045 (output weight -9.44)

Region values of the output score: x0 < -0.5 -> about -8.7 (class 0); -0.5 < x0 < 0.5 -> about +8.9
(class 1); x0 > 0.5 -> about -9.1 (class 0). h1_0 and h1_3 are complementary steps at the same point
(one up, one down), and h1_1/h1_2 are two near-duplicate steps at 0.5 that merge into one.
The exact decision boundaries (by a fine scan) are x0 = -0.4942 and x0 = 0.5020.
